<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:frechet-error}
Under Conditions~\hyperref[par:M1]{\textnormal{(M1)}}, \hyperref[par:M2]{\textnormal{(M2)}}--\hyperref[par:M3]{\textnormal{(M3)}}, \hyperref[par:K1]{\textnormal{(K1)}}--\hyperref[par:K3]{\textnormal{(K3)}},
\hyperref[par:R1]{\textnormal{(R1)}}--\hyperref[par:R2]{\textnormal{(R2)}}, and \eqref{eq:basic-tuning-regime}, suppose either
that $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}>0$ and $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}\to0$, or
that an exact measurable minimizer is available and
$\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}=0$. Then
\[
\begin{split}
    \mathfrak R_{s,\xi,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}}
    =O_p\big(\rho_{r,s,n,N,\xi}^{\,2/(\beta_M-1)}
      +\rho_{r,s,n,N,\xi}^{\,2}
      +\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}^{2/\beta_M}\big).
\end{split}
\]
In particular, $\rho_{r,s,n,N,\xi}\to0$ implies consistency.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:sharp-frechet-error}
Under Conditions~\hyperref[par:M1]{\textnormal{(M1)}}, \hyperref[par:M2]{\textnormal{(M2)}}--\hyperref[par:M3]{\textnormal{(M3)}}, \hyperref[par:K1]{\textnormal{(K1)}},
\hyperref[par:K4]{\textnormal{(K4)}}--\hyperref[par:K5]{\textnormal{(K5)}}, \hyperref[par:R1]{\textnormal{(R1)}}--\hyperref[par:R2]{\textnormal{(R2)}}, and
\eqref{eq:sharp-tuning-regime}, the conclusion of
Theorem~\ref{thm:frechet-error} holds with
$\rho_{r,s,n,N,\xi}^{\star}$ in place of $\rho_{r,s,n,N,\xi}$,
under the same minimizer convention.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:polynomial-rate}
Assume Conditions~\hyperref[par:M1]{\textnormal{(M1)}}, \hyperref[par:M2]{\textnormal{(M2)}}--\hyperref[par:M3]{\textnormal{(M3)}}, \hyperref[par:K1]{\textnormal{(K1)}},
\hyperref[par:K4]{\textnormal{(K4)}}--\hyperref[par:K5]{\textnormal{(K5)}}, and \hyperref[par:R1]{\textnormal{(R1)}}--\hyperref[par:R2]{\textnormal{(R2)}}, and the minimizer convention
of Theorem~\ref{thm:frechet-error}. If $qr>2$, $N\ge n$, and
\[
    s\asymp n^{1/(qr)},\qquad
    0<\xi=O\big(s^{-(3q-1)/2}\big),
\]
then \eqref{eq:sharp-tuning-regime} holds and
\[
\begin{split}
    \mathfrak R_{s,\xi,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}}
    =O_p\big(n^{-\frac{qr-1}{qr(\beta_M-1)}}
       +n^{-\frac{qr-1}{qr}}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}^{2/\beta_M}\big).
\end{split}
\]
For $\beta_M=2$, this becomes
$O_p(n^{-(qr-1)/(qr)}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}})$.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:ph-balanced-rate}
Under Corollary~\ref{cor:polynomial-rate} with Hadamard responses, fit
both endpoints at its fixed common kernel, rank, ridge and tolerance, and use exact
PH interpolation. For any measurable, possibly validation-selected
$\widehat\eta\in[0,1]$,
\[
 \mathfrak R(\widehat m^{\mathrm{PH}}_{\widehat\eta})
 =O_p\big(n^{-(qr-1)/(qr)}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}\big).
\]
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:th-stability}
Under the common-tuning conventions above, assume the conditions of
Theorem~\ref{thm:frechet-error} or \ref{thm:sharp-frechet-error}, with
$\rho_{\mathrm N}=\rho_{r,s,n,N,\xi}$ or
$\rho_{r,s,n,N,\xi}^{\star}$, respectively.
Suppose $\mathcal Y$ is Hadamard. There is one nonnegative
$Z_{n,N}=O_p(1)$, common to all mixing weights, such that
simultaneously for all $(\alpha_{\mathrm H},\eta)\in[0,1]^2$,
{\@setsize\@setsize\@setsize\@setsize\@setsize\@setsize\@setsize\@setsize\small{10pt}\ixpt\@ixpt{10pt}\ixpt\@ixpt{10pt}\ixpt\@ixpt{10pt}\ixpt\@ixpt{10pt}\ixpt\@ixpt{10pt}\ixpt\@ixpt{10pt}\ixpt\@ixpt{10pt}\ixpt\@ixpt
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:th-stability-bound}
\begin{aligned}
 &\mathfrak R(\widehat m_{\alpha_{\mathrm H},\eta}^{\mathrm{TH}})
   +\mathfrak D(\widehat m_{\alpha_{\mathrm H},\eta}^{\mathrm{TH}},\widehat m^{\mathrm N})\\
 &\quad\le Z_{n,N}\left(\rho_{\mathrm N}^2
       +(1-\alpha_{\mathrm H}\eta)^2\chi_{s,n,N,\xi}^2
       +\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{hyb}}\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
}
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:app-pd-kernel-rkhs}
A symmetric function
$\kappa:\mathcal S\times\mathcal S\to\mathbb R$ is a \emph{positive definite kernel} if,
for every $m\ge1$, $x_1,\ldots,x_m\in\mathcal S$, and
$c_1,\ldots,c_m\in\mathbb R$,
\[
    \sum_{a=1}^m\sum_{b=1}^m
    c_ac_b\kappa(x_a,x_b)\ge0.
\]
A Hilbert space $\mathcal H_\kappa$ of real-valued functions on $\mathcal S$ is the
\emph{reproducing kernel Hilbert space} associated with $\kappa$ if
$K_x:=\kappa(x,\cdot)\in\mathcal H_\kappa$ for every $x\in\mathcal S$ and
\[
    f(x)
    =\langle f,K_x\rangle_{\mathcal H_\kappa},
    \qquad
    f\in\mathcal H_\kappa.
\]
Every positive definite kernel determines a unique RKHS.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:app-centered-rkhs-realization}
The function $\kappa_0$ is a continuous positive definite kernel.  Its RKHS
is realized as follows.  Let
\[
    \mathcal H_0
    :=\overline{\operatorname{span}}
      \{K_x-m_\kappa:x\in\mathcal S\}
      \subseteq\mathcal H_\kappa
\]
and define $\mathcal J:\mathcal H_0\to\mathbb R^{\mathcal S}$ by
$(\mathcal J w)(x)=\langle w,K_x^0\rangle_{\mathcal H_\kappa}$.
Then $\mathcal J$ is an isometry of $\mathcal H_0$ onto
$\mathcal H_{\kappa_0}$. Under this identification, the function
$\mathcal J(K_x^0)=\kappa_0(x,\cdot)$ is the representer of evaluation and
\begin{align*}
    f(x)
    &=\langle f,K_x^0\rangle_{\mathcal H_{{\kappa_0}}},\\
    \mathbb E[K_X^0]
    &=0,
    \qquad
    \mathbb E[f(X)]=0
    \quad
    \text{for every }f\in\mathcal H_{{\kappa_0}},\\
    \kappa_0(x,x)
    &=\|K_x^0\|_{\mathcal H_{{\kappa_0}}}^2,\\
    \|K_x^0-K_{x'}^0\|_{\mathcal H_{{\kappa_0}}}^2
    &=
    \kappa_0(x,x)+\kappa_0(x',x')-2\kappa_0(x,x').
 \end{align*}
Moreover, with the ambient bound $B_\kappa:=\sup_{x\in\mathcal X}\kappa(x,x)$
from Condition~\nameref{par:K1},
\[
    \sup_{x\in\mathcal S}\kappa_0(x,x)
    \le4B_\kappa.
\]
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
The displayed formula for $\kappa_0$ is a feature-map representation, so
$\kappa_0$ is positive definite.  Continuity follows from continuity of
$x\mapsto K_x$.  If $\mathcal J w=0$, then $w$ is orthogonal to every $K_x^0$ and
hence to $\mathcal H_0$, so $w=0$. Thus $\mathcal J$ is injective. Equip
\(\mathcal J(\mathcal H_0)\) with the inner product transported from \(\mathcal H_0\),
so that
\(\langle \mathcal J w,\mathcal J v\rangle_{\mathcal J(\mathcal H_0)}
  :=\langle w,v\rangle_{\mathcal H_\kappa}\).
Then \(\mathcal J\) is an isometry and \(\mathcal J(\mathcal H_0)\) is complete. Moreover,
\[
 \langle \mathcal J(K_x^0),\mathcal J(K_{x'}^0)\rangle_{\mathcal J(\mathcal H_0)}
 =\langle K_x^0,K_{x'}^0\rangle_{\mathcal H_\kappa}
 =\kappa_0(x,x').
\]
To make this identification explicit, recall that the Moore--Aronszajn
construction starts from finite linear combinations of the kernel sections
\(\kappa_0(x,\cdot)\), equips them with the inner product
\[
 \left\langle
   \sum_{i=1}^m c_i\kappa_0(x_i,\cdot),
   \sum_{j=1}^{\ell}d_j\kappa_0(y_j,\cdot)
 \right\rangle
 :=
 \sum_{i=1}^m\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}
 c_id_j\kappa_0(x_i,y_j),
\]
identifies elements whose difference has zero norm, and takes the
Hilbert-space completion.  Here
\(\mathcal J(K_x^0)=\kappa_0(x,\cdot)\), and the definition of \(\mathcal H_0\)
shows that
\[
 \mathcal J(\mathcal H_0)
 =
 \overline{\operatorname{span}}
 \{\kappa_0(x,\cdot):x\in\mathcal S\}
\]
in the transported norm. Thus \(\mathcal J(\mathcal H_0)\) is exactly the complete
space produced by this construction and is canonically isometric to
\(\mathcal H_{\kappa_0}\). Moreover, for \(w\in\mathcal H_0\),
\[
 (\mathcal J w)(x)
 =
 \langle w,K_x^0\rangle_{\mathcal H_\kappa}
 =
 \langle \mathcal J w,\mathcal J(K_x^0)\rangle_{\mathcal J(\mathcal H_0)},
\]
which gives the displayed reproducing identity. Since
$\mathbb E[K_X^0]=\mathbb E[K_X]-m_\kappa=0$, taking inner products with
$f\in\mathcal H_{{\kappa_0}}$ gives the displayed mean-zero identities.  The
remaining identities follow from the Hilbert-space norm, and
\[
    \|K_x^0\|_{\mathcal H_\kappa}
    \le
    \|K_x\|_{\mathcal H_\kappa}+\|m_\kappa\|_{\mathcal H_\kappa}
    \le2\sqrt{B_\kappa},
\]
which proves the stated bound.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
\label{ex:fourier-kernel-moment-spectrum}
Let $\mathcal X=\mathbb T:=\mathbb R/(2\pi\mathbb Z)$ with its usual circular
metric, and let $\mathbb P_X$ be the uniform probability measure. For $m\ge1$, put
\[
    \phi_{2m-1}(\theta)=\sqrt2\cos(m\theta),\qquad
    \phi_{2m}(\theta)=\sqrt2\sin(m\theta).
\]
These functions form an orthonormal basis of $L^2_0(\mathbb P_X)$ and have zero
mean. For any $q>1$, define
\[
    \kappa(\theta,\theta')
    :=1+\sum_{j\ge1}j^{-q}\phi_j(\theta)\phi_j(\theta').
\]
Since $|\phi_j(\theta)\phi_j(\theta')|\le2$ and
$\sum_jj^{-q}<\infty$, the series converges absolutely and uniformly.
Each summand is a positive semidefinite kernel, so $\kappa$ is continuous,
positive definite, and bounded by
$\sup_\theta\kappa(\theta,\theta)\le1+2\sum_jj^{-q}<\infty$.
Thus Condition~\hyperref[par:K1]{\textnormal{(K1)}} holds. Population centering removes precisely the
constant term, and orthonormality gives the positive eigenpairs
$(\lambda_j,\phi_j)$ with $\lambda_j=j^{-q}$.
Consequently,
\[
    \sup_{j\ge1}\mathbb E|\phi_j(X)|^8\le16,
    \qquad
    \lambda_j-\lambda_k=j^{-q}-k^{-q}\quad(j<k).
\]
Condition~\hyperref[par:K4]{\textnormal{(K4)}} holds, Condition~\hyperref[par:K5]{\textnormal{(K5)}} holds with equality in its
cross-boundary comparison, and every positive integer cutoff satisfies
Condition~\hyperref[par:K2]{\textnormal{(K2)}}. This example verifies only the kernel-side conditions;
the response conditions and tuning restrictions remain separate.
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- example-2: body_md -->
\label{ex:bhv-closure-source}
Consider unrooted four-taxon BHV trees with fixed pendant lengths. Their
internal-edge space is a tripod: three rays $R_1,R_2,R_3$ meeting at an
unresolved star tree $o$ \citep{billera2001geometry}. Denote radius $a\ge0$
on ray $k$ by $aR_k$. The intrinsic distance is $|a-b|$ on the same ray
and $a+b$ on distinct rays.

Take the circular covariate and Fourier kernel in
Example~\ref{ex:fourier-kernel-moment-spectrum} with $q=4$. Thus
$X\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\mathbb T)$, $\lambda_j=j^{-4}$, and
$\overline{\mathcal H_{\kappa_0}}^{\,L^2(\mathbb P_X)}=L_0^2(\mathbb P_X)$. Put $u=\sin x$ and let
\[
 T(x)=\begin{cases}
       uR_1,&u\ge0,\\
       (-u)R_2,&u<0.
       \end{cases}
 \qquad Y=W T(X),\qquad
 W\sim\operatorname{Unif}[1/2,3/2],\quad W\perp X.
\]
Scaling changes only the internal-edge length. Conditional on $X=x$,
the response lies on a single ray, has mean radius $|u|$, and has
Fr\'echet mean $m_\oplus(x)=T(x)$. In particular $o$ belongs to the target
image. For the candidate $y=aR_3$, $a>0$,
\[
 M_\oplus(y,x)=a^2+\mathbb E[W^2]\sin^2x+2a|\sin x|,\qquad
 M_\oplus(o,x)=\mathbb E[W^2]\sin^2x.
\]
Consequently the normalized centered contrast is
\[
 \dot h_{(o,aR_3)}(x)=2\big(|\sin x|-2/\pi\big).
\]
This is a nonsmooth function of the \emph{covariate}; merely observing
nonsmoothness in the response candidate would not establish
nonmembership in a covariate RKHS.

The Fourier identity
\[
 |\sin x|=\frac2\pi-\frac4\pi
     \sum_{m\ge1}\frac{\cos(2mx)}{4m^2-1}
\]
shows that this centered contrast has coefficients of order $m^{-2}$
along $\phi_{4m-1}=\sqrt2\cos(2mx)$. Thus its RKHS squared norm would
contain a divergent series of order
$\sum_m m^{-4}(4m-1)^4$. It does not belong to
$\mathcal H_{\kappa_0}$, even as an $L^2$ equivalence class with an RKHS
representative, but it does belong to $\overline{\mathcal H_{\kappa_0}}^{\,L^2(\mathbb P_X)}$.

This example also verifies the \emph{uniform} fractional source rather
than only one contrast. For a point of radius $a$ on ray $k$, write
\[
 F_1(u)=u,\qquad F_2(u)=-u,\qquad F_3(u)=-|u|.
\]
Its squared distance to $WT(x)$ is
$a^2+W^2u^2-2aW F_k(u)$. In the contrast between $y=aR_k$ and
$z=bR_\ell$, the $W^2u^2$ term cancels. If $k=\ell$, division by
$|a-b|$ gives a coefficient of magnitude two; if $k\ne\ell$, division
by $a+b$ gives two coefficients with total absolute value two.
After marginal centering, every contrast therefore has the form
\[
 \dot h_t(x)=\alpha_t\sin x+\beta_t\big(|\sin x|-2/\pi\big),\qquad
 |\alpha_t|+|\beta_t|\le2.
\]
The constant $\beta_t$ here is a coefficient, not the curvature exponent
$\beta_M$. The weighted coefficient sums are uniformly finite whenever
$4r<3$, while $\sum_j\lambda_j^r<\infty$ whenever $4r>1$.
Thus \hyperref[par:R1]{\textnormal{(R1)}} holds for every $1/4<r<3/4$, although its exact-RKHS
case fails.

Conditions \hyperref[par:M1]{\textnormal{(M1)}} and \hyperref[par:M2]{\textnormal{(M2)}} hold on the whole circle. The tripod
is Hadamard, so \hyperref[par:M3]{\textnormal{(M3)}} holds with $\beta_M=2$. Moreover
\[
 \zeta_t(x,WT(x))=(W-1)\{\alpha_t\sin x+\beta_t|\sin x|\}.
\]
For conditionally independent radii, Cauchy--Schwarz in $\mathbb R^2$
and conditional centering give \hyperref[par:R2]{\textnormal{(R2)}} with
$C_M^2\le8\operatorname{Var}(W)$. The kernel conditions
\hyperref[par:K1]{\textnormal{(K1)}}, \hyperref[par:K2]{\textnormal{(K2)}}, \hyperref[par:K4]{\textnormal{(K4)}}, and \hyperref[par:K5]{\textnormal{(K5)}} hold by
Example~\ref{ex:fourier-kernel-moment-spectrum}.
For instance, $r=5/8$ gives $qr=5/2>2$ and the refined source rate
$O_p(n^{-3/5}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}})$ with the tuning in
Corollary~\ref{cor:polynomial-rate}.

The absolute value arises from crossing branches and comparing with a
third branch; two-ray distances alone can cancel it. Smooth mixture
probabilities or other conditional laws can also smooth a Fr\'echet
function. Hence BHV geometry alone does not imply failure of RKHS
membership. This constructed example verifies the new assumptions; it
is not an assumption verification for every experimental BHV mechanism.
<!-- end example-2 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
A geodesic metric space $(\mathcal Y,d_{\mathcal Y})$ is a \emph{CAT(0) space} if, for
all $y_0,y_1,v\in\mathcal Y$, every constant-speed geodesic
$\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathcal Y$ from $y_0$ to $y_1$ satisfies
\[
    d_{\mathcal Y}(v,\gamma(t))^2
    \le
    (1-t)d_{\mathcal Y}(v,y_0)^2
    +t d_{\mathcal Y}(v,y_1)^2
    -t(1-t)d_{\mathcal Y}(y_0,y_1)^2,
    \qquad t\in[0,1].
\]
A \emph{Hadamard space} is a complete CAT(0) space.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
A Borel probability measure $P$ on $\mathcal Y$ has a \emph{finite second
moment} if
\[
    \int_{\mathcal Y}d_{\mathcal Y}(y,y_0)^2\,dP(y)<\infty
\]
for some, and hence every, $y_0\in\mathcal Y$.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:automatic-quadratic-curvature}
Under Condition~\nameref{par:M1}, the conditional law
$\mathbb P_{Y\mid X=x}$ has a finite second moment for every $x\in\mathcal X_0$. The following
assertions hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=*]
\item If $\mathcal Y$ is a Hilbert space, then for every $x\in\mathcal X_0$ and $y\in\mathcal Y$,
\[
    \Delta_\oplus(y,x)
    =\|y-m_\oplus(x)\|^2.
\]
\item If $\mathcal Y$ is a Hadamard space, then for every $x\in\mathcal X_0$ and $y\in\mathcal Y$,
\[
    \Delta_\oplus(y,x)
    \ge d_\mathcal Y(y,m_\oplus(x))^2.
\]
\end{enumerate}
Consequently, in either case the local curvature part of
Condition~\nameref{par:M3} holds globally in $y$, uniformly for
$x\in\mathcal X_0$, with \(\beta_M=2\) and \(c_M=1\).
The same inequality also implies uniform identification, because for every
$\eta>0$,
\[
    \inf_{x\in\mathcal X_0}\inf_{d_\mathcal Y(y,m_\oplus(x))\ge\eta}
    \Delta_\oplus(y,x)\ge\eta^2.
\]
Thus Condition~\nameref{par:M1}, together with Hilbert or Hadamard geometry,
supplies the curvature and identification parts of
Condition~\nameref{par:M3}; Condition~\nameref{par:M2} records the
measurability of the chosen conditional-mean or conditional-barycenter
version.
The RKHS approximation and response-noise complexity requirements are handled
separately by Conditions~\nameref{par:R1}--\nameref{par:R2}.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Fix $x\in\mathcal X_0$. In the Hilbert case, put $m=\mathbb E[Y\mid X=x]$.  Since
$\mathbb E[Y-m\mid X=x]=0$, expansion of the square gives
\[
\begin{split}
 M_\oplus(y,x)
 &=\mathbb E[\|y-m+m-Y\|^2\mid X=x]\\
 &=\|y-m\|^2+\mathbb E[\|Y-m\|^2\mid X=x].
\end{split}
\]
Taking $y=m$ and subtracting proves part~(i). For the Hadamard case, put
$m=m_\oplus(x)$, and let $\gamma_t$ be the geodesic from $m$ to $y$.
The CAT(0) variance inequality \citep{sturm2003probability} gives
\[
    M_\oplus(\gamma_t,x)
    \le (1-t)M_\oplus(m,x)+tM_\oplus(y,x)
       -t(1-t)d_\mathcal Y(m,y)^2.
\]
Since $m$ minimizes the conditional Fr\'echet function,
$M_\oplus(m,x)\le M_\oplus(\gamma_t,x)$.  Dividing by $t>0$ yields
\[
    \Delta_\oplus(y,x)
    \ge (1-t)d_\mathcal Y(m,y)^2.
\]
Letting $t\downarrow0$ proves part~(ii).
The Hilbert conditional mean and the Hadamard barycenter exist uniquely
because Condition~\nameref{par:M1} gives finite conditional second moments.
Finally, the displayed global bound
implies the uniform-identification display in the statement.  Measurability
of $x\mapsto m_\oplus(x)$ is retained explicitly in
Condition~\nameref{par:M2}, since it concerns the chosen version of the
regular conditional law.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:coercivity}
For every $x\in\mathcal X$, the map
\[
    y\longmapsto\widehat M_{s,\xi}(y,x)
    =\sum_{i\in L}a_{s,\xi,i}(x)d_{\mathcal Y}^2(y,Y_i)
\]
is coercive: it diverges whenever $d_{\mathcal Y}(y_*,y)\to\infty$ for any fixed
$y_*\in\mathcal Y$.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Fix $x\in\mathcal X$ and a reference point $y_0\in\mathcal Y$.
The reverse triangle inequality gives
\[
    |d_{\mathcal Y}(y,Y_i)-d_{\mathcal Y}(y,y_0)|
    \le d_{\mathcal Y}(Y_i,y_0).
\]
Consequently,
\[
    |d_{\mathcal Y}^2(y,Y_i)-d_{\mathcal Y}^2(y,y_0)|
    \le
    d_{\mathcal Y}(Y_i,y_0)
    \{2d_{\mathcal Y}(y,y_0)+d_{\mathcal Y}(Y_i,y_0)\}.
\]
Since $\sum_{i\in L}a_{s,\xi,i}(x)=1$,
\[
\begin{split}
    \widehat M_{s,\xi}(y,x)
    ={}&d_{\mathcal Y}^2(y,y_0)
    +\sum_{i\in L}a_{s,\xi,i}(x)
      \{d_{\mathcal Y}^2(y,Y_i)-d_{\mathcal Y}^2(y,y_0)\}\\
    \ge{}&d_{\mathcal Y}^2(y,y_0)
    -2d_{\mathcal Y}(y,y_0)
      \sum_{i\in L}|a_{s,\xi,i}(x)|d_{\mathcal Y}(Y_i,y_0)
    -\sum_{i\in L}|a_{s,\xi,i}(x)|d_{\mathcal Y}^2(Y_i,y_0).
\end{split}
\]
For fixed data and fixed $x$, the two sums on the right-hand side are
finite constants. Hence
\[
    \widehat M_{s,\xi}(y,x)\longrightarrow\infty
    \qquad\text{as }d_{\mathcal Y}(y,y_0)\longrightarrow\infty,
\]
which proves coercivity.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:measurable-approximate-minimizer}
For the estimated Fr\'echet function in \eqref{eq:ss-krfr-criterion}, for every
$\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}>0$, there exists a selector jointly measurable
in the observed data and $x$ satisfying
\eqref{eq:ss-krfr-estimator}. If an exact measurable argmin selector exists,
one may take $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}=0$.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Let \(\mathcal Y_0=\{y_1,y_2,\ldots\}\) be a fixed countable dense subset of
\(\mathcal Y\), and fix \(y_0\in\mathcal Y\). The empirical matrix is a Borel function of
\(\mathbf d\), and the fixed Borel eigensystem rule specified in
Section~\ref{app:empirical-spectral-construction} makes every empirical
feature a jointly Borel function of \((\mathbf d,x)\). Thus, for every fixed
\(j\), the map
\((\mathbf d,x)\mapsto\widehat M_{s,\xi}(y_j,x;\mathbf d)\) is measurable.
The lower bound from the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:coercivity} gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
    \widehat M_{s,\xi}(y,x;\mathbf d)
    &\ge
    d_{\mathcal Y}^2(y,y_0)
    -2d_{\mathcal Y}(y,y_0)
      \sum_{i\in L}|a_{s,\xi,i}(x;\mathbf d)|
      d_{\mathcal Y}(v_i,y_0)
    -\sum_{i\in L}|a_{s,\xi,i}(x;\mathbf d)|d_{\mathcal Y}^2(v_i,y_0)\\
    &\ge
    -\left\{
      \sum_{i\in L}|a_{s,\xi,i}(x;\mathbf d)|
      d_{\mathcal Y}(v_i,y_0)
    \right\}^2
    -\sum_{i\in L}|a_{s,\xi,i}(x;\mathbf d)|d_{\mathcal Y}^2(v_i,y_0).
\end{aligned}
\]
For fixed \((\mathbf d,x)\), the two sums in the final bound are finite.
Together with finiteness at \(y_1\), this shows that the infimum over \(y\)
is a finite real number. Hence
\[
    q(\mathbf d,x):=\inf_{\ell\ge1}
      \widehat M_{s,\xi}(y_\ell,x;\mathbf d)
\]
is jointly measurable as a countable infimum. By the definition of an infimum,
for every $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}>0$ the set
\[
    \left\{j:\widehat M_{s,\xi}(y_j,x;\mathbf d)
    \le q(\mathbf d,x)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}\right\}
\]
is nonempty. Let \(\widehat j(\mathbf d,x)\) be its smallest element. Moreover,
\[
    \{(\mathbf d,x):\widehat j(\mathbf d,x)=j\}
    =\{(\mathbf d,x):\widehat M_{s,\xi}(y_j,x;\mathbf d)
          \le q(\mathbf d,x)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}\}
       \cap\bigcap_{\ell<j}
       \{(\mathbf d,x):\widehat M_{s,\xi}(y_\ell,x;\mathbf d)
          >q(\mathbf d,x)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}\},
\]
so \(\widehat j\), and therefore
\(\widehat m_{s,\xi,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}}(x;\mathbf d)
:=y_{\widehat j(\mathbf d,x)}\), is jointly measurable.

For fixed $(\mathbf d,x)$, the objective function is continuous in $y$. Since
$\mathcal Y_0$ is dense,
\[
    \inf_{\ell\ge1}\widehat M_{s,\xi}(y_\ell,x;\mathbf d)
    =\inf_{y\in\mathcal Y}\widehat M_{s,\xi}(y,x;\mathbf d).
\]
The asserted approximate optimality follows from the definition of
\(\widehat j(\mathbf d,x)\).
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor:proper}
If $(\mathcal Y,d_{\mathcal Y})$ is proper, that is, every closed ball is compact, then
$\operatorname*{argmin}_{y\in\mathcal Y}\widehat M_{s,\xi}(y,x)$ is nonempty.
Thus, there is an exact selector jointly measurable in the observed data and
$x$, so one may take $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}=0$.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Fix $\mathbf d\in\mathbb D_{n,N}$ and $x$. The map
$y\mapsto\widehat M_{s,\xi}(y,x;\mathbf d)$ is continuous. By coercivity,
the sublevel set
\[
    K_{\mathbf d,x}:=\{y:\widehat M_{s,\xi}(y,x;\mathbf d)
       \le \widehat M_{s,\xi}(y_1,x;\mathbf d)+1\}
\]
is bounded. It is closed by continuity and compact since $\mathcal Y$ is proper. The global infimum equals the infimum over $K_{\mathbf d,x}$, so
continuity and compactness give a nonempty compact argmin.

For joint measurability, use the countable dense set
$\mathcal Y_0=\{y_1,y_2,\ldots\}$ and define
\[
    q(\mathbf d,x):=\inf_{j\ge1}
       \widehat M_{s,\xi}(y_j,x;\mathbf d).
\]
Continuity in $y$ shows that this equals the infimum over all $y\in\mathcal Y$, and
the countable representation makes $q$ measurable. Since the weights and response coordinates are
Borel and $y\mapsto d_\mathcal Y^2(y,v_i)$ is continuous, the objective function is jointly
Borel in $(\mathbf d,x,y)$. Hence the argmin graph
\[
 \mathcal G
 :=\{(\mathbf d,x,y):
   \widehat M_{s,\xi}(y,x;\mathbf d)=q(\mathbf d,x)\}
\]
is Borel and has nonempty closed sections. The space
$\mathbb D_{n,N}\times\mathcal X$ is standard Borel, $\mathcal Y$ is Polish, and the
sections are compact by the first part of the proof. The Arsenin--Kunugui
uniformization theorem \citep{kechris2012classical} therefore yields a
jointly Borel exact selector.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{cor:Hilbert}
If $\mathcal Y$ is a Hilbert space, then the empirical minimizer is unique and
\[
    \widehat m_{s,\xi,0}(x)
    =\sum_{i\in L}a_{s,\xi,i}(x)Y_i.
\]
Thus $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}=0$ is available.
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Since $\sum_{i\in L}a_{s,\xi,i}(x)=1$, expansion of the squared Hilbert
norm gives
\[
    \widehat M_{s,\xi}(y,x)
    =\left\|y-\sum_{i\in L}a_{s,\xi,i}(x)Y_i\right\|_{\mathcal Y}^2+C(x),
\]
where $C(x)$ is independent of $y$. The first term is strictly convex and
has the unique minimizer displayed in the corollary. The weights and the
responses are measurable, so this exact minimizer is measurable and one may
set $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}=0$.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:directional-algebra}
For every $t=(z,y)\in\mathfrak T$ and $v\in\mathcal Y$,
\[
    d_{\mathcal Y}^2(y,v)-d_{\mathcal Y}^2(z,v)
    =d_t^2+d_t\psi_t(v),
\]
and
\[
    |\psi_t(v)|\le2d_{\mathcal Y}(z,v).
\]
Consequently, for $x\in\mathcal X_0$,
\[
    \dot C_\oplus(t;x)=d_t+H_t(x)
    =d_t+\overline\psi_t+\dot h_t(x).
\]
For any weights $a_1,\ldots,a_n$ satisfying $\sum_i a_i=1$,
\[
    \frac{
        \sum_i a_i d_{\mathcal Y}^2(y,v_i)
        -\sum_i a_i d_{\mathcal Y}^2(z,v_i)
    }{d_t}
    =
    d_t+\sum_i a_i\psi_t(v_i).
\]
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
The first identity is the definition of $\psi_t$. Let
$r=d_{\mathcal Y}(y,z)$ and $a=d_{\mathcal Y}(z,v)$. The triangle inequality gives
$|a-r|\le d_{\mathcal Y}(y,v)\le a+r$. Squaring these bounds gives
\[
    -2ar
    \le
    d_{\mathcal Y}^2(y,v)-a^2-r^2
    \le
    2ar,
\]
which proves the envelope after division by $r$. Taking conditional
expectations proves the population identity. The weighted identity follows
by summing the first display and using $\sum_i a_i=1$.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
Under Conditions~\nameref{par:M1} and \nameref{par:M2}, put
\[
    \sigma_*^2
    :=
    \sup_{x\in\mathcal X_0}M_\oplus(y_*,x)<\infty.
\]
For \(t=(z,y)\in\mathfrak T\), the first coordinate satisfies
\(z=m_\oplus(x_z)\) for some \(x_z\in\mathcal X_0\). The minimizing property gives
\[
    \mathbb E[d_{\mathcal Y}^2(z,Y)\mid X=x_z]
    \le
    \mathbb E[d_{\mathcal Y}^2(y_*,Y)\mid X=x_z]
    \le\sigma_*^2.
\]
Minkowski's inequality therefore yields
\[
    d_{\mathcal Y}(z,y_*)\le2\sigma_*.
\]
For any \(x\in\mathcal X_0\),
\[
\begin{split}
    \mathbb E[d_{\mathcal Y}^2(z,Y)\mid X=x]
    &\le
    2d_{\mathcal Y}^2(z,y_*)
    +2\mathbb E[d_{\mathcal Y}^2(y_*,Y)\mid X=x]\\
    &\le10\sigma_*^2.
\end{split}
\]
Combining this bound with Lemma~\ref{lem:directional-algebra} gives
\[
    \mathbb E[\psi_t(Y)^2\mid X=x]
    \le4\mathbb E[d_{\mathcal Y}^2(z,Y)\mid X=x]
    \le40\sigma_*^2,
\]
uniformly in \(t\in\mathfrak T\) and \(x\in\mathcal X_0\). Hence the
unconditional means $\overline\psi_t$ are finite, and $H_t$, $\zeta_t$,
and the conditional definition of $\dot h_t$ are well-defined on
$\mathcal X_0$ with the measurable versions fixed in the main text.
Condition~\nameref{par:R1} supplies fractional-source control of the
centered conditional contrasts in the fixed $L^2$ closure; only their
finite spectral projections need be RKHS functions.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:population-rkhs-surrogate}
Under \hyperref[par:K1]{\textnormal{(K1)}} and \hyperref[par:R1]{\textnormal{(R1)}}, write
$a_j(t)=\lambda_j^{-r/2}c_j(t)$. Then
\[
 \dot h_t=\sum_{j\ge1}\lambda_j^{r/2}a_j(t)\phi_j
       \quad\hbox{in }L^2(\mathbb P_X),\qquad
 \sup_t\sum_j a_j(t)^2\le B_r^2.
\]
The finite projection $g_{t,s}=\Pi_{\le s}\dot h_t$ belongs to
$\mathcal H_{\kappa_0}$, with
\[
 \sup_t\|g_{t,s}\|_{\mathcal H_{\kappa_0}}^2
 =\sup_t\sum_{j\le s}a_j(t)^2\lambda_j^{r-1}
 \le B_r^2\lambda_s^{r-1}=G_{r,s}^2.
\]
The infinite series need not converge in the RKHS when $r<1$.
Under the additional moment and target conditions \hyperref[par:M1]{\textnormal{(M1)}} and \hyperref[par:M2]{\textnormal{(M2)}},
all displayed scalar expansions can be made simultaneous on the common
full-measure set $\mathcal X_0$, after a Borel full-measure restriction if needed.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
The positive eigenfunctions form an orthonormal basis of
$\overline{\mathcal H_{\kappa_0}}^{\,L^2(\mathbb P_X)}$.
Closure membership and Parseval give the $L^2$ expansion. Condition
\hyperref[par:R1]{\textnormal{(R1)}} gives the coefficient bound. Since $0<r\le1$ and
$\lambda_j\ge\lambda_s$ for $j\le s$, the finite RKHS norm is bounded
as displayed; no RKHS norm is assigned to the infinite target.

For the version assertion additionally use \hyperref[par:M1]{\textnormal{(M1)}} and \hyperref[par:M2]{\textnormal{(M2)}}.
Tonelli and \hyperref[par:R1]{\textnormal{(R1)}} give a Borel full-measure
set on which $\sum_j\lambda_j^r\phi_j(x)^2<\infty$. On this set,
Cauchy--Schwarz gives, uniformly in $t$,
\[
 \left|\sum_{j>J}\lambda_j^{r/2}a_j(t)\phi_j(x)\right|
 \le B_r\left(\sum_{j>J}\lambda_j^r\phi_j(x)^2\right)^{1/2}\longrightarrow0.
\]
The series therefore converges uniformly in $t$ at each such $x$.
The chosen conditional contrasts are continuous in $t=(z,y)$ on
$\mathfrak T$ with its product metric: the contrast is pointwise continuous
off the diagonal, and Lemma~\ref{lem:directional-algebra} and the uniform
bound $d_{\mathcal Y}(z,y_*)\le2\sigma_*$ give an integrable dominating function,
both conditionally at any $x\in\mathcal X_0$ and unconditionally. The same bound
makes $c_j(t)$ continuous by dominated convergence. Thus both the
conditional contrast and its spectral series are continuous in $t$.
Choose a countable dense subset of the original index set, intersect the
sets of $L^2$ agreement for its elements, and then use continuity to
extend agreement to every $t$. Intersecting with the preceding Borel
set gives the claimed common full-measure restriction. The moment,
identification, and curvature conditions are preserved by this
restriction, and any resulting smaller target index set inherits all
bounds. The stipulated measurable, separable versions are retained.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:automatic-spectral-truncation}
Under \hyperref[par:M1]{\textnormal{(M1)}}, \hyperref[par:M2]{\textnormal{(M2)}}, \hyperref[par:K1]{\textnormal{(K1)}}, and \hyperref[par:R1]{\textnormal{(R1)}}, put
\[
 v_{r,s}(x):=(\lambda_j^{r/2}\phi_j(x))_{j>s}\in\ell^2,
 \qquad r_{t,s}:=\dot h_t-g_{t,s}.
\]
On the common full-measure set,
\[
 r_{t,s}(x)=\langle a_{>s}(t),v_{r,s}(x)\rangle_{\ell^2},\qquad
 \|v_{r,s}\|_{L^2(\mathbb P_X;\ell^2)}^2=\sum_{j>s}\lambda_j^r.
\]
Consequently
\[
 \left\|\sup_t|r_{t,s}|\right\|_{L^2(\mathbb P_X)}\le b_{r,s}\longrightarrow0.
\]
If $\lambda_j\asymp j^{-q}$, summability is equivalent to $qr>1$ and
$b_{r,s}^2\lesssim s^{-(qr-1)}$.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
The first identity follows from Lemma~\ref{lem:population-rkhs-surrogate}.
Tonelli and $\mathbb E\phi_j(X)^2=1$ give the vector-valued norm identity.
Taking the supremum in Cauchy--Schwarz before integrating gives the
bound with $B_r$. Summability yields its limit. Integral comparison of
$\sum_{j>s}j^{-qr}$ proves the polynomial assertion.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:hilbert-automatic-complexity}
Let $\mathcal Y=\mathcal H_Y$ be a separable Hilbert space and assume \hyperref[par:M1]{\textnormal{(M1)}}.
Then \hyperref[par:R2]{\textnormal{(R2)}} holds without any source assumption. For source regularity,
let $0<r\le1$, $\sum_j\lambda_j^r<\infty$, and suppose
\[
 m_c:=\mathbb E[Y\mid X=\cdot]-\mathbb E Y
       =\sum_{j\ge1}\theta_j\phi_j
       \quad\hbox{in }L^2(\mathbb P_X;\mathcal H_Y),\qquad
 M_r^2:=\sum_{j\ge1}\frac{\|\theta_j\|_{\mathcal H_Y}^2}{\lambda_j^r}<\infty.
\]
Then \hyperref[par:R1]{\textnormal{(R1)}} holds with any fixed $B_r\ge2M_r$ and the appropriate
common versions. In the contrast and prediction bounds one may replace
$b_{r,s}$ by
\[
 b^{\mathrm H}_{r,s}:=2\left(\sum_{j>s}\|\theta_j\|_{\mathcal H_Y}^2\right)^{1/2}
 \le2M_r\lambda_{s+1}^{r/2},
\]
while using $G^{\mathrm H}_{r,s}:=2M_r\lambda_s^{(r-1)/2}$ in the ridge
and representation terms (or the larger $G_{r,s}$). The nondegenerate
case $M_r>0$ is understood in the latter notation; if $M_r=0$, the
centered signal terms are zero. For $\lambda_j\asymp j^{-q}$,
$(b^{\mathrm H}_{r,s})^2\lesssim s^{-qr}$.
The conclusions also hold for a closed convex subset of a separable
Hilbert space with its induced distance.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
For $u_t=(y-z)/\|y-z\|$, the squared-norm identity gives
\[
 \psi_t(v)=-2\langle u_t,v-z\rangle,\qquad
 \dot h_t(x)=-2\langle u_t,m_c(x)\rangle,
 \qquad c_j(t)=-2\langle u_t,\theta_j\rangle.
\]
Hence $\sum_j c_j(t)^2/\lambda_j^r\le4M_r^2$, uniformly in $t$, and
closure membership follows from the vector expansion. The scalar
versions are justified by Lemma~\ref{lem:population-rkhs-surrogate};
equivalently the vector series converges absolutely in norm almost
everywhere by the source bound and $\sum_j\lambda_j^r\phi_j(x)^2<\infty$.
For the residual, define the deterministic Hilbert-valued tail
$V_s(x)=\sum_{j>s}\theta_j\phi_j(x)$. Orthogonality gives
\[
 \|V_s\|_{L^2(\mathbb P_X;\mathcal H_Y)}^2
 =\sum_{j>s}\|\theta_j\|^2
 \le \lambda_{s+1}^r M_r^2.
\]
Moreover $r_{t,s}=-2\langle u_t,V_s\rangle$ and
$\mathbb E P_L\|V_s\|^2=\|V_s\|_{L^2}^2$. Markov's inequality and the
Hilbert-valued smoother bound of Lemma~\ref{lem:hilbert-smoother-stability}
give $\|\widehat S V_s\|_{L^2}=O_p(\|V_s\|_{L^2})$. The triangle
inequality and $\sup_t\|u_t\|=1$ therefore yield
\[
 \left\|\sup_t|(\widehat S-I)r_{t,s}|\right\|_2
 =O_p\big(b^{\mathrm H}_{r,s}\big).
\]
This proves the claimed replacement for the fitted residual, not merely
a pointwise approximation bound. The finite-source norm bound is the
one already proved, with $B_r=2M_r$.

To check \hyperref[par:R2]{\textnormal{(R2)}}, condition on $x_1,\ldots,x_k$ and set
$\epsilon_i=Y_i-\mathbb E[Y_i\mid X=x_i]$. Then
\[
 \sup_t\left|\sum_iw_i\zeta_t(x_i,Y_i)\right|
 \le2\left\|\sum_iw_i\epsilon_i\right\|_{\mathcal H_Y}.
\]
Conditional independence and centering give
\[
 \mathbb E\left[\left\|\sum_iw_i\epsilon_i\right\|^2\,\middle|\,x_1,\ldots,x_k\right]
 =\sum_iw_i^2\mathbb E[\|\epsilon_i\|^2\mid X=x_i]
 \le\sigma_Y^2\sum_iw_i^2,
\]
where $\sigma_Y^2=\sup_{x\in\mathcal X_0}\mathbb E[\|Y-y_*\|^2\mid X=x]<\infty$.
Thus $C_M=2\sigma_Y$ suffices. If the response is restricted to a closed
convex subset, its Bochner conditional mean remains in that subset,
all distance identities above are unchanged, and the same argument
applies. This includes the closed convex quantile representation of
univariate $W_2$ responses \citep{bigot2017geodesic}.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{prop:metric-response-entropy}
Suppose that $(\mathfrak T,d_\zeta)$ is a separable totally bounded
semimetric space, fix $t_0\in\mathfrak T$, and write
$D_\zeta=\operatorname{diam}(\mathfrak T,d_\zeta)$. Assume that the processes
below have measurable $d_\zeta$-separable versions. Assume also that,
uniformly in $x\in\mathcal X_0$,
\begin{align}
 \|\zeta_t(x,Y)-\zeta_{t'}(x,Y)\|_{\psi_2(\mathbb P_{Y\mid X=x})}
 &\le L_\zeta d_\zeta(t,t'),
 \label{eq:metric-entropy-increment}\\
 \|\zeta_{t_0}(x,Y)\|_{\psi_2(\mathbb P_{Y\mid X=x})}
 &\le \sigma_\zeta,
 \notag
\end{align}
and that
\[
 J_\zeta
 :=\int_0^{D_\zeta}
 \sqrt{1+\log N(\epsilon,\mathfrak T,d_\zeta)}\,d\epsilon
 <\infty.
\]
Then Condition~\nameref{par:R2} holds with
\[
 C_M\le C\{\sigma_\zeta+L_\zeta J_\zeta\},
\]
where $C$ is numerical. In particular,
the entropy integral is finite if, for some finite $v>0$ and
$A\ge D_\zeta$,
\[
 \log N(\epsilon,\mathfrak T,d_\zeta)
 \le v\log(A/\epsilon),\qquad 0<\epsilon\le D_\zeta.
\]

Alternatively, the abstract hypotheses can be verified directly from the
response spaces as follows. Suppose that $(\mathcal M,d_\mathcal Y)$ and
$(\mathcal Y,d_\mathcal Y)$ are totally bounded, with diameters $D_\mathcal M$ and $D_\mathcal Y$, and
give $\mathfrak T\subset\mathcal M\times\mathcal Y$ the product semimetric
\[
 d_\oplus((z,y),(z',y'))
 :=d_\mathcal Y(z,z')+d_\mathcal Y(y,y').
\]
Assume that the processes have measurable $d_\oplus$-separable versions and,
for some $t_0\in\mathfrak T$, uniformly in $x\in\mathcal X_0$,
\[
 \|\zeta_t(x,Y)-\zeta_{t'}(x,Y)\|_{\psi_2(\mathbb P_{Y\mid X=x})}
 \le L_\oplus d_\oplus(t,t'),
 \qquad
 \|\zeta_{t_0}(x,Y)\|_{\psi_2(\mathbb P_{Y\mid X=x})}
 \le\sigma_\oplus.
\]
If
\[
 J_\mathcal M+J_\mathcal Y
 :=\int_0^{D_\mathcal M}\!\sqrt{1+\log N(\epsilon,\mathcal M,d_\mathcal Y)}\,d\epsilon
 +\int_0^{D_\mathcal Y}\!\sqrt{1+\log N(\epsilon,\mathcal Y,d_\mathcal Y)}\,d\epsilon
 <\infty,
\]
then Condition~\nameref{par:R2} holds with
\[
 C_M\le C\{\sigma_\oplus+L_\oplus(J_\mathcal M+J_\mathcal Y)\}.
\]
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Fix $x_1,\ldots,x_k\in\mathcal X_0$ and $\mathbf w\in\mathbb R^k$, and condition on
these covariates. Put
\[
 Z_t:=\sum_{i=1}^k w_i\zeta_t(x_i,Y_i).
\]
The summands are conditionally independent and centered. The standard
Orlicz-norm inequality for sums of independent centered sub-Gaussian random
variables and \eqref{eq:metric-entropy-increment} give
\[
 \|Z_t-Z_{t'}\|_{\psi_2}
 \le C\left\{\sum_{i=1}^k w_i^2
 \|\zeta_t(x_i,Y_i)-\zeta_{t'}(x_i,Y_i)\|_{\psi_2}^2\right\}^{1/2}
 \le CL_\zeta\|\mathbf w\|_2d_\zeta(t,t').
\]
Likewise,
$\|Z_{t_0}\|_{\psi_2}\le C\sigma_\zeta\|\mathbf w\|_2$.
Let $d_Z(t,t'):=\|Z_t-Z_{t'}\|_{\psi_2}$. The preceding display gives
$d_Z\le CL_\zeta\|\mathbf w\|_2d_\zeta$. We give the required $L^2$ chaining
calculation explicitly. Put $D=D_\zeta$.
If $D=0$, then $d_Z(t,t_0)=0$ for every $t$ and the assumed separable
version gives $Z_t=Z_{t_0}$ almost surely on the determining subclass, so
the desired bound is immediate. Hence suppose $D>0$, put
$\delta_m=2^{-m}D$, and let $T_m$
be a finite $\delta_m$-net of $\mathfrak T$ with
$|T_m|=N(\delta_m,\mathfrak T,d_\zeta)$, take $T_0=\{t_0\}$, and choose
$\pi_m:\mathfrak T\to T_m$ such that
$d_\zeta(t,\pi_m(t))\le\delta_m$, with $\pi_0(t)=t_0$.

For centered sub-Gaussian variables $V_1,\ldots,V_M$, put
$\sigma_M:=\bigvee_{r\le M}\|V_r\|_{\psi_2}$. If $\sigma_M=0$, all the
variables vanish almost surely and the bound below is immediate. Otherwise,
the union bound gives
\[
 \mathbb P\!\left(\bigvee_{r\le M}|V_r|>u\right)
 \le2M\exp\!\left\{-\frac{cu^2}{\sigma_M^2}\right\},
\]
and integration of
$\mathbb E\bigvee_{r\le M}|V_r|^2
=\int_0^\infty 2u\,\mathbb P(\bigvee_{r\le M}|V_r|>u)\,du$,
split at $u_0=C\sigma_M\sqrt{1+\log M}$, implies
\[
 \left\|\bigvee_{r\le M}|V_r|\right\|_{L^2}
 \le C\sqrt{1+\log M}
       \bigvee_{r\le M}\|V_r\|_{\psi_2}.
\]
Apply this finite maximum bound at level $m$ to the increments
$Z_u-Z_v$ indexed by pairs
$(u,v)=(\pi_m(t),\pi_{m-1}(t))$ that occur as $t$ varies. There are at
most $|T_m||T_{m-1}|$ such pairs, and their $d_\zeta$-length is at most
$\delta_m+\delta_{m-1}$. Since
$N(\delta_{m-1},\mathfrak T,d_\zeta)
\le N(\delta_m,\mathfrak T,d_\zeta)$, it follows that
\[
 \left\|\bigvee_{t\in\mathfrak T}
 |Z_{\pi_m(t)}-Z_{\pi_{m-1}(t)}|\right\|_{L^2}
 \le C L_\zeta\|\mathbf w\|_2\delta_{m-1}
 \sqrt{1+\log N(\delta_m,\mathfrak T,d_\zeta)}.
\]
For
$f(\epsilon):=\sqrt{1+\log N(\epsilon,\mathfrak T,d_\zeta)}$,
monotonicity of the covering number gives, term by term,
\[
 \delta_{m-1}f(\delta_m)
 =2\delta_m f(\delta_m)
 \le4\int_{\delta_{m+1}}^{\delta_m}f(\epsilon)\,d\epsilon.
\]
Therefore,
\[
 \sum_{m\ge1}\delta_{m-1}
 \sqrt{1+\log N(\delta_m,\mathfrak T,d_\zeta)}
 \le
 C\int_0^D
 \sqrt{1+\log N(\epsilon,\mathfrak T,d_\zeta)}\,d\epsilon
 =CJ_\zeta.
\]
For any finite subset $F$ of the fixed countable determining subclass, the
telescoping identity for $Z_{\pi_M(t)}-Z_{t_0}$, Minkowski's inequality, and
$d_Z(\pi_M(t),t)\to0$ yield, upon first letting $M\to\infty$,
\[
 \left\|\bigvee_{t\in F}|Z_t-Z_{t_0}|\right\|_{L^2}
 \le CL_\zeta\|\mathbf w\|_2J_\zeta.
\]
Letting $F$ increase to that determining subclass and using monotone
convergence proves
\[
 \left\|\,\sup_{t\in\mathfrak T}|Z_t-Z_{t_0}|\right\|_{L^2}
 \le CL_\zeta\|\mathbf w\|_2J_\zeta.
\]
This is the $L^2$ form of the usual sub-Gaussian entropy bound. The triangle
inequality and the base-point bound prove the asserted abstract
constant bound. Finally,
$\int_0^{D_\zeta}\sqrt{1+v\log(A/\epsilon)}\,d\epsilon<\infty$, proving the
polynomial-entropy assertion.

For the response-space specialization, form the product of an
$\epsilon/4$-net of $\mathcal M$ and an $\epsilon/4$-net of $\mathcal Y$. In each product
cell that intersects $\mathfrak T$, choose one point of the intersection as
its center. Every point in that cell is within \(\epsilon\) of the chosen
center in \(d_\oplus\). Therefore, even when covering centers are required to
belong to \(\mathfrak T\),
\[
 N(\epsilon,\mathfrak T,d_\oplus)
 \le
 N(\epsilon/4,\mathcal M,d_\mathcal Y)
 N(\epsilon/4,\mathcal Y,d_\mathcal Y).
\]
Since $\sqrt{1+a+b}\le\sqrt{1+a}+\sqrt{1+b}$ for $a,b\ge0$ and
$\operatorname{diam}(\mathfrak T,d_\oplus)\le D_\mathcal M+D_\mathcal Y$, splitting the
integral at the two diameters and changing variables gives
\[
 \int_0^{\operatorname{diam}(\mathfrak T,d_\oplus)}
 \sqrt{1+\log N(\epsilon,\mathfrak T,d_\oplus)}\,d\epsilon
 \le C(J_\mathcal M+J_\mathcal Y).
\]
Apply the abstract part with $d_\zeta=d_\oplus$ to obtain
the asserted response-space constant bound.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
The local entropy assumptions used in global and local Fr\'echet regression
have the form
\[
 \int_0^1\sqrt{1+\log N(\delta\epsilon,
 B_\delta(m_\oplus(x)),d_{\mathcal Y})}\,d\epsilon=O(1),
\]
pointwise or uniformly in the predictor
\citep{petersen2019frechet,chen2022uniform}.  Proposition~
\ref{prop:metric-response-entropy} is the corresponding condition for the
object actually indexed in the present proof: the normalized conditional
noise class $\{\zeta_t:t\in\mathfrak T\}$. The response-space specialization
above makes the transfer explicit when the normalized contrast map is
uniformly Lipschitz in $d_\oplus$ and the two global entropy integrals are
finite. This response-space route is a convenient sufficient verification.
Since the normalized contrast divides by $d_{\mathcal Y}(y,z)$, a global
product-metric Lipschitz bound can be restrictive as $y$ approaches $z$. In
such settings the abstract $d_\zeta$ condition can be verified directly.
Local covering arguments can also be paired with localization before they
imply the global maximal inequality in Condition~\nameref{par:R2}. The
Hilbert proposition above gives a separate verification.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:fixed-design-decomposition}
On $\mathcal E_s$, write
$\ell_{t,s}=\widehat\Pi_{\le s}g_{t,s}$,
$d_{t,s}=(I-\widehat\Pi_{\le s})g_{t,s}$, and
$\mathbf c_{t,s}=\widehat E_s^*g_{t,s}$. For $x\in\mathcal X_0$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:directional-fixed-design-decomposition}
\begin{split}
 \widehat C_{s,\xi}(t;x)-\dot C_\oplus(t;x)
 ={}&\sum_{i\in L}a_{s,\xi,i}(x)\zeta_t(X_i,Y_i)
       +(\widehat S-I)r_{t,s}(x)\\
    &+(\widehat S-I)d_{t,s}(x)
       -\xi\mathbf q_L(x)^\top\mathbf A_s\mathbf c_{t,s}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
The weights reproduce constants, so the difference is the first term
plus $(\widehat S-I)\dot h_t$. Use
$\dot h_t=r_{t,s}+d_{t,s}+\ell_{t,s}$. In the common RKHS,
\[
 \ell_{t,s}(x)=\widehat{\boldsymbol\Phi}_s(x)^\top\mathbf c_{t,s}
       +\langle\ell_{t,s},\overline K_N^0\rangle.
\]
The constant is absorbed by the unpenalized intercept. Centered ridge
therefore gives
\[
 \widehat S\ell_{t,s}(x)=P_L\ell_{t,s}
       +\mathbf q_L(x)^\top\mathbf A_s\mathbf C_{L,s}\mathbf c_{t,s}
       =\ell_{t,s}(x)-\xi\mathbf q_L(x)^\top\mathbf A_s\mathbf c_{t,s}.
\]
This proves the identity. In particular, the learned-subspace component
is reproduced exactly at zero ridge on an invertible design; no
independent labeled signal-fluctuation term is introduced.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{prop:primitive-spectral-verification}
Under Conditions~\nameref{par:K1}--\nameref{par:K3},
$\mathbb P(\mathcal E_s)\to1$, and the full-sample empirical eigensystem satisfies
\[
    \frac{\widehat\lambda_s}{\lambda_s}\xrightarrow{p}1,
    \qquad
    \|\mathbf B_s^{-1/2}\overline{\mathbf C}_s\mathbf B_s^{-1/2}\|_\mathrm{op}=O_p(1),
\]
\[
    \|\overline K_N^0\|_{\mathcal H_{\kappa_0}}=O_p(N^{-1/2}),
\]
and
\[
    \|\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}^{1/2}(\widehat\Pi_{\le s}-\Pi_{\le s})\|_\mathrm{HS}
    =O_p\!\left(
      \frac{1}
      {\sqrt N\,(\lambda_s-\lambda_{s+1})}
    \right)
    =o_p(1).
\]
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Write $Z_i=K_{X_i}^0$ and
\[
    \widetilde{\mathcal T}_N:=\frac1N\sum_{i=1}^NZ_i\otimes Z_i,
    \qquad
    \Delta_N:=\widehat{\mathcal T}_{\kappa_0,N}-\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}
    =(\widetilde{\mathcal T}_N-\mathcal T_{\kappa_0})
      -\overline K_N^0\otimes\overline K_N^0.
\]
Condition~\nameref{par:K1} and Proposition~
\ref{prop:app-centered-rkhs-realization} give
$\|Z_i\|^2\le C_\kappa<\infty$. Independence in the Hilbert space of
Hilbert--Schmidt operators therefore yields
\[
 \mathbb E\|\widetilde{\mathcal T}_N-\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
 =\frac1N\mathbb E\|Z\otimes Z-\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
 \le\frac{C}{N}.
\]
Also, because $\mathbb E Z=0$,
\[
    \mathbb E\|\overline K_N^0\|^2
    =\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\mathcal T_{\kappa_0})}{N}.
\]
Consequently,
\[
    \|\Delta_N\|_\mathrm{op}=O_p(N^{-1/2}),
    \qquad
    \|\overline K_N^0\|=O_p(N^{-1/2}).
\]
Condition~\nameref{par:K3} states
$N^{-1/2}=o(\lambda_s-\lambda_{s+1})$. Combining it with the covariance
bound above gives
\[
    \frac{\|\Delta_N\|_\mathrm{op}}
    {\lambda_s-\lambda_{s+1}}=o_p(1).
\]
Weyl's inequality now gives, with probability tending to one,
\[
    \widehat\lambda_s-\widehat\lambda_{s+1}
    \ge \lambda_s-\lambda_{s+1}-2\|\Delta_N\|_\mathrm{op}>0.
\]
This proves $\mathbb P(\mathcal E_s)\to1$. Moreover,
$|\widehat\lambda_s-\lambda_s|\le\|\Delta_N\|_\mathrm{op}$; since
$\lambda_s-\lambda_{s+1}\le\lambda_s$, it follows that
$\widehat\lambda_s/\lambda_s\to_p1$.

On the same event, the Davis--Kahan theorem gives
\[
 \|\widehat\Pi_{\le s}-\Pi_{\le s}\|_\mathrm{op}
 \le\frac{C\|\Delta_N\|_\mathrm{op}}
 {\lambda_s-\lambda_{s+1}}.
\]
Put $A=\widehat\Pi_{\le s}-\Pi_{\le s}$. Since $A$ is self-adjoint,
$A^2\preceq\|A\|_\mathrm{op}^2\mathrm{Id}$, and therefore
\[
\begin{split}
 \|\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}^{1/2}A\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
 &=\operatorname{tr}(A\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}A)
   =\operatorname{tr}\{\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}^{1/2}A^2
   \mathcal T_{\kappa_0}^{1/2}\}\\
 &\le \|A\|_\mathrm{op}^2\operatorname{tr}(\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}).
\end{split}
\]
Hence
\[
 \|\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}^{1/2}(\widehat\Pi_{\le s}-\Pi_{\le s})\|_\mathrm{HS}
 =O_p\!\left(
   \frac{1}{\sqrt N\,(\lambda_s-\lambda_{s+1})}
 \right).
\]
The exceptional-event convention preserves this $O_p$ conclusion.

It remains to verify the pooled query bound. On $\mathcal E_s$, for every
$\mathbf a\in\mathbb R^s$, put $h=\widehat E_s\mathbf a$. By
\eqref{eq:supp-overline-C},
\[
\begin{split}
 \mathbf a^\top\overline{\mathbf C}_s\mathbf a
 &=\langle h,(\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}
     +\overline K_N^0\otimes\overline K_N^0)h\rangle\\
 &\le \mathbf a^\top\widehat{\boldsymbol\Lambda}_s\mathbf a
   +(\|\Delta_N\|_\mathrm{op}+\|\overline K_N^0\|^2)\|\mathbf a\|^2.
\end{split}
\]
The last coefficient is $o_p(\lambda_s)$, while
$\widehat\lambda_s/\lambda_s\to_p1$. Thus, with probability tending to one,
$\overline{\mathbf C}_s\preceq C\widehat{\boldsymbol\Lambda}_s$, and consequently
\[
    \|\mathbf B_s^{-1/2}\overline{\mathbf C}_s\mathbf B_s^{-1/2}\|_\mathrm{op}\le C.
\]
The centered labeled inverse is controlled separately below.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:cross-boundary-spectrum-consequences}
Under Conditions~\nameref{par:K2} and \nameref{par:K5}, the following bounds hold at every
selected complete spectral-block cutoff $s$:
\begin{align}
    \mathcal N(\lambda_{s+1})
    &\lesssim s,
    \label{eq:derived-effective-dimension}\\
    \mathfrak r_s^{\partial}
    &\lesssim s\log(es),
    \label{eq:derived-block-relative-complexity}\\
    \mathcal U_s
    &\lesssim s^2\log(es),
    \label{eq:derived-unweighted-projector-sum}\\
    \mathcal W_s^{+}
    &\lesssim s^{2-q}\log(es).
    \label{eq:derived-leading-pca-excess-scale}
\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
For $s=1$, Condition~\nameref{par:K5} gives
$\lambda_1-\lambda_k\gtrsim1-k^{-q}$ and $\lambda_k\lesssim k^{-q}$ for
$k>1$. Since $q>1$, the series defining all four quantities are bounded by
constants. It therefore suffices to consider $s\ge2$.
Polynomial decay gives
$\lambda_s\asymp\lambda_{s+1}\asymp s^{-q}$. Therefore
\[
 \mathcal N(\lambda_{s+1})
 \le s+\lambda_{s+1}^{-1}\sum_{k>s}\lambda_k
 \lesssim s+s^q\sum_{k>s}k^{-q}
 \lesssim s,
\]
where the last step uses $q>1$.

For the relative rank, Condition~\nameref{par:K5} gives
\[
 \mathfrak r_s^{\partial}
 \lesssim
 \sum_{j\le s}\frac{j^{-q}}{j^{-q}-(s+1)^{-q}}
 +
 \sum_{k>s}\frac{k^{-q}}{s^{-q}-k^{-q}}.
\]
If $j\le s/2$, the first summand is $O(1)$.  If $s/2<j\le s$,
the mean-value theorem gives
\[
 j^{-q}-(s+1)^{-q}
 \ge c_q(s+1-j)s^{-q-1},
\]
so the summand is at most $Cs/(s+1-j)$.  Similarly, the second
summand is at most $Cs/(k-s)$ for $s<k\le2s$, while
\[
 \sum_{k>2s}\frac{k^{-q}}{s^{-q}-k^{-q}}
 \lesssim s^q\sum_{k>2s}k^{-q}
 \lesssim s.
\]
Summing the two harmonic series proves
\eqref{eq:derived-block-relative-complexity}.

For the one-sided excess scale, polynomial decay and the relative-rank
calculation above give
\[
 \sum_{k>s}\lambda_k\lesssim s^{1-q},
 \qquad
 \sum_{j\le s}\frac{\lambda_j}{\lambda_j-\lambda_{s+1}}
 \lesssim s\log(es).
\]
Multiplication proves
\eqref{eq:derived-leading-pca-excess-scale}.

It remains to treat $\mathcal U_s$.  Split the cross-boundary
index set into
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal I_1&:=\{j\le s/2,\ k>s\},\\
 \mathcal I_2&:=\{s/2<j\le s,\ k>2s\},\\
 \mathcal I_3&:=\{s/2<j\le s<k\le2s\}.
\end{aligned}
\]
On $\mathcal I_1\cup\mathcal I_2$, $k\ge2j$ and hence
$j^{-q}-k^{-q}\ge(1-2^{-q})j^{-q}$.  Consequently,
\[
 \frac{\lambda_j\lambda_k}{(\lambda_j-\lambda_k)^2}
 \lesssim j^q k^{-q}.
\]
Integral comparison then gives
\[
\begin{split}
 \sum_{\mathcal I_1\cup\mathcal I_2}j^qk^{-q}
 &\lesssim
 \left(\sum_{j\le s}j^q\right)
 \left(\sum_{k>s}k^{-q}\right)
 \lesssim s^2.
\end{split}
\]

On $\mathcal I_3$, all eigenvalues are comparable to $s^{-q}$ and the
relative-gap condition plus the mean-value theorem gives
\[
 \lambda_j-\lambda_k
 \gtrsim j^{-q}-k^{-q}
 \gtrsim(k-j)s^{-q-1}.
\]
Also $\lambda_j-\lambda_{s+1}\le\lambda_j\lesssim s^{-q}$. Hence
\[
 \frac{\lambda_j\lambda_k}{(\lambda_j-\lambda_k)^2}
 \lesssim\frac{s^2}{(k-j)^2}.
\]
Writing $a=s+1-j$ and $b=k-s$ shows
\[
 \sum_{\mathcal I_3}\frac1{(k-j)^2}
 \le
 \sum_{a=1}^{s}\sum_{b=1}^{s}
 \frac1{(a+b-1)^2}
 \lesssim\log(es).
\]
Combining the three regions proves
\eqref{eq:derived-unweighted-projector-sum}.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:tuning-consequences}
Under Conditions~\nameref{par:K2}, \nameref{par:K5}, and
\eqref{eq:sharp-tuning-regime},
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:tuning-effective-dimension-smallness}
    \mathfrak r_s^{\partial}\sqrt{\frac{\log N}{N}}\longrightarrow0,
    \qquad
    \frac{\mathcal N(\lambda_{s+1})+\log N}{N}\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
The following moment-based smallness conditions also hold:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:tuning-moment-smallness}
    \frac{\mathcal N(\lambda_{s+1})}{\sqrt N}\longrightarrow0,
    \qquad
    \frac{(\mathfrak r_s^{\partial})^2}{N}\longrightarrow0,
    \qquad
    \frac{s^2}{N(\log N)^2}\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Lemma~\ref{lem:cross-boundary-spectrum-consequences} gives
$\mathcal N(\lambda_{s+1})\lesssim s$ and
$\mathfrak r_s^{\partial}\lesssim s\log(es)$. The first restriction in
\eqref{eq:sharp-tuning-regime} therefore gives the first limit in
\eqref{eq:tuning-effective-dimension-smallness}. Its square states
\[
    \frac{s^2\log^2(es)\log N}{N}\longrightarrow0.
\]
Since $N\to\infty$ and $\log(es)\ge1$, this implies all three limits in
\eqref{eq:tuning-moment-smallness}. Finally,
$\mathcal N(\lambda_{s+1})/N\lesssim s/N\le s/n\to0$ and
$\log N/N\to0$, proving the remaining limit.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:spectral-transform-moments}
Assume Condition~\nameref{par:K4}. Write
$\mathcal T=\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}$, $Z=K_X^0$, and
$e_j=\sqrt{\lambda_j}\phi_j$ in the fixed eigenbasis satisfying that
condition. Let $Z_1,\ldots,Z_N$ be independent copies of $Z$, and put
\[
    \widetilde{\mathcal T}_N=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^N Z_i\otimes Z_i,
    \qquad
    \bar Z_N=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^N Z_i,
    \qquad
    \widehat{\mathcal T}_N
      =\widetilde{\mathcal T}_N-\bar Z_N\otimes\bar Z_N.
\]
Let $B$ be a fixed bounded self-adjoint operator diagonal in this
basis, with $Be_j=\beta_j e_j$, and define
\[
    b(B):=\operatorname{tr}(B\mathcal T B)
         =\sum_{j\ge1}\lambda_j\beta_j^2<\infty.
\]
There is a constant $C$ depending only on $C_{X,8}$ such that
\begin{align}
    \mathbb E\|BZ\|^4
      &\le Cb(B)^2,
      \label{eq:spectral-transform-fourth-moment}\\
    \mathbb E\|B(\widetilde{\mathcal T}_N-\mathcal T)B\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
      &\le \frac{Cb(B)^2}{N},
      \label{eq:spectral-transform-covariance-moment}\\
    \mathbb E\|B\bar Z_N\|^2
      &=\frac{b(B)}{N},\qquad
    \mathbb E\|B\bar Z_N\|^4\le\frac{Cb(B)^2}{N^2},
      \label{eq:spectral-transform-mean-moments}\\
    \mathbb E\|B(\widehat{\mathcal T}_N-\mathcal T)B\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
      &\le\frac{Cb(B)^2}{N}.
      \label{eq:spectral-transform-centered-moment}
\end{align}
In particular, the operator norms of the two transformed covariance
perturbations are $O_p(b(B)/\sqrt N)$, and
$\|B\bar Z_N\|^2=O_p(b(B)/N)$. These bounds hold uniformly for fixed
choices of $B$ that may depend on $n,N,s$; no uniform bound on $\|B\|_\mathrm{op}$
across sample sizes is needed. This does not assert a bound for data-dependent
choices of $B$ or for a supremum over transforms. If $b(B)=0$, all the
displayed quantities vanish almost surely.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Put $\eta_j=\lambda_j^{-1/2}\langle e_j,Z\rangle=\phi_j(X)$. Then
$\mathbb E\eta_j=0$, $\mathbb E\eta_j^2=1$, and
\[
    \sup_j\mathbb E|\eta_j|^4\le C_{X,8}^{1/2}=:C_4.
\]
The coordinates need not be independent. With
$a_j=\lambda_j\beta_j^2$, the spectral expansion of $Z$ and Tonelli's
theorem give
\[
\begin{split}
    \mathbb E\|BZ\|^4
    &=\sum_{j,k\ge1}a_ja_k\mathbb E(\eta_j^2\eta_k^2)\\
    &\le\sum_{j,k\ge1}a_ja_k
          (\mathbb E\eta_j^4\,\mathbb E\eta_k^4)^{1/2}
     \le C_4 b(B)^2.
\end{split}
\]
Independence of the observations in the Hilbert space of Hilbert--Schmidt
operators now yields
\[
\begin{split}
    \mathbb E\|B(\widetilde{\mathcal T}_N-\mathcal T)B\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
    &=\frac1N\mathbb E\|BZ\otimes BZ-B\mathcal T B\|_\mathrm{HS}^2\\
    &\le\frac1N\mathbb E\|BZ\|^4.
\end{split}
\]
Since $\mathbb E Z=0$, the second-moment identity for the sample mean gives
$\mathbb E\|B\bar Z_N\|^2=b(B)/N$.

For its fourth moment, put
$\bar\eta_j=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^N\eta_j(X_i)$. Independence and centering
across observations give the exact identity
\[
    \mathbb E\bar\eta_j^4
    =N^{-4}\{N\mathbb E\eta_j^4+3N(N-1)\}
    \le\frac{C}{N^2}.
\]
Thus $\mathbb E(\bar\eta_j^2\bar\eta_k^2)\le C/N^2$ by
Cauchy--Schwarz. Tonelli's theorem then gives
\[
    \mathbb E\|B\bar Z_N\|^4
    =\sum_{j,k\ge1}a_ja_k
         \mathbb E(\bar\eta_j^2\bar\eta_k^2)
    \le\frac{Cb(B)^2}{N^2}.
\]
All infinite sums used here have nonnegative summands, so the same
calculations apply to the infinite-dimensional space without any exchange of
conditionally convergent series.

Finally,
\[
    B(\widehat{\mathcal T}_N-\mathcal T)B
    =B(\widetilde{\mathcal T}_N-\mathcal T)B
       -(B\bar Z_N)\otimes(B\bar Z_N),
\]
and the squared Hilbert--Schmidt norm of the rank-one term is
$\|B\bar Z_N\|^4$. The squared triangle inequality proves
\eqref{eq:spectral-transform-centered-moment}. Markov's inequality and
$\|\cdot\|_\mathrm{op}\le\|\cdot\|_\mathrm{HS}$ give the asserted stochastic orders.
The constants in these moment bounds do not depend on the diagonal
coefficients of $B$, which justifies their use for sample-size-dependent
transforms chosen without using the observations. When $b(B)=0$, $\mathbb E\|BZ\|^2=0$, and all the
conclusions are immediate.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:derived-covariance-stability}
Under Conditions~\nameref{par:K2}, \nameref{par:K4}--\nameref{par:K5}, and
\eqref{eq:sharp-tuning-regime}, put $\mu=\lambda_{s+1}$. Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:derived-preconditioned-stability}
    \chi_{s,N}
    :=\left\|
        (\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}+\mu\mathrm{Id})^{-1/2}
        (\widetilde{\mathcal T}_N-\mathcal T_{\kappa_0})
        (\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}+\mu\mathrm{Id})^{-1/2}
      \right\|_\mathrm{op}
    =O_p\!\left(\frac{\mathcal N(\mu)}{\sqrt N}\right)
    =o_p(1),
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:derived-centering-rate}
    \|\overline K_N^0\|=O_p(N^{-1/2}),
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:derived-regularized-mean-stability}
    \nu_{s,N}
    :=\left\|
       (\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}+\mu\mathrm{Id})^{-1/2}\overline K_N^0
      \right\|^2
    =O_p\!\left(\frac{\mathcal N(\mu)}{N}\right)
    =o_p(1).
\end{equation}
Consequently, with $\delta_{s,N}:=\chi_{s,N}+\nu_{s,N}=o_p(1)$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:derived-centered-preconditioned-stability}
 \left\|
   (\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}+\mu\mathrm{Id})^{-1/2}
   (\widehat{\mathcal T}_{\kappa_0,N}-\mathcal T_{\kappa_0})
   (\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}+\mu\mathrm{Id})^{-1/2}
 \right\|_\mathrm{op}
 \le\delta_{s,N}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
The population operator
$B_\mu=(\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}+\mu\mathrm{Id})^{-1/2}$ is bounded for
each $s$ and diagonal in the fixed population eigenbasis. Its transformed
covariance trace is
\[
    b(B_\mu)
    =\operatorname{tr}\{\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}
           (\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}+\mu\mathrm{Id})^{-1}\}
    =\mathcal N(\mu).
\]
Lemma~\ref{lem:spectral-transform-moments} therefore gives
\[
    \mathbb E\chi_{s,N}^2\le\frac{C\mathcal N(\mu)^2}{N},
    \qquad
    \mathbb E\nu_{s,N}=\frac{\mathcal N(\mu)}{N}.
\]
Markov's inequality gives the displayed stochastic orders, and
Lemma~\ref{lem:tuning-consequences} makes both quantities $o_p(1)$.
These conclusions use only coordinatewise moments, not a uniform tail
bound for arbitrary linear combinations of eigenfunctions.
Also,
\[
    \mathbb E\|\overline K_N^0\|^2
    =\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\mathcal T_{\kappa_0})}{N},
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:derived-centering-rate}. Finally,
$\widehat{\mathcal T}_{\kappa_0,N}
 =\widetilde{\mathcal T}_N-\overline K_N^0\otimes\overline K_N^0$,
and the regularized rank-one correction has operator norm $\nu_{s,N}$.
The triangle inequality proves
\eqref{eq:derived-centered-preconditioned-stability}.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:empirical-eigenvalue-comparability}
Under Conditions~\nameref{par:K2}, \nameref{par:K4}--\nameref{par:K5}, and
\eqref{eq:sharp-tuning-regime},
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:empirical-eigenvalue-comparability}
    \bigvee_{1\le j\le s+1}
    \left|\frac{\widehat\lambda_j}{\lambda_j}-1\right|
    =o_p(1).
\end{equation}
In particular, $\widehat\lambda_s>0$ with probability tending to one.
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Put $\mu=\lambda_{s+1}$ and use $\delta_{s,N}$ from
Lemma~\ref{lem:derived-covariance-stability}. On the event
$\delta_{s,N}<1/4$, \eqref{eq:derived-centered-preconditioned-stability}
gives the quadratic-form bounds
\[
 (1-\delta_{s,N})\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}-\delta_{s,N}\mu\mathrm{Id}
 \preceq\widehat{\mathcal T}_{\kappa_0,N}
 \preceq(1+\delta_{s,N})\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}
          +\delta_{s,N}\mu\mathrm{Id}.
\]
For $j\le s+1$, the min--max principle and $\mu\le\lambda_j$ imply
\[
 (1-2\delta_{s,N})\lambda_j
 \le(1-\delta_{s,N})\lambda_j-\delta_{s,N}\mu
 \le\widehat\lambda_j
 \le(1+\delta_{s,N})\lambda_j+\delta_{s,N}\mu
 \le(1+2\delta_{s,N})\lambda_j.
\]
Since $\delta_{s,N}=o_p(1)$, this proves the assertion for the raw
empirical eigenvalues, including repeated eigenvalues.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:empirical-cutoff-separation}
Under Conditions~\nameref{par:K2}, \nameref{par:K4}--\nameref{par:K5}, and
\eqref{eq:sharp-tuning-regime},
\[
    \mathbb P\!\left(\widehat\lambda_s-\widehat\lambda_{s+1}
       \ge\tfrac34(\lambda_s-\lambda_{s+1})\right)\longrightarrow1,
    \qquad \mathbb P(\mathcal E_s)\longrightarrow1.
\]
Put
\[
    \mu_s=\lambda_{s+1},\qquad
    g_s=\lambda_s-\lambda_{s+1},\qquad
    \Delta_N=\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}-\widehat{\mathcal T}_{\kappa_0,N},\qquad
    S_s=\sum_{j\le s}(\lambda_j-\mu_s)^{-1/2}\Pi_j.
\]
The following bounds also hold:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:centered-pca-eigenvalue-good-event}
    \mathbb P\!\left(\widehat\lambda_{s+1}-\mu_s>g_s/2\right)
    \le\frac{C(\mathfrak r_s^\partial)^2}{N}=o(1),
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:centered-pca-weighted-good-event}
    \mathbb P\{\|S_s\Delta_NS_s\|_\mathrm{op}\le1/4\}
    \ge1-\frac{C(\mathfrak r_s^\partial)^2}{N}\longrightarrow1.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
Write $\mathcal T=\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}$ and define the midpoint and its
spectral transform by
\[
    c_s:=\frac{\lambda_s+\lambda_{s+1}}2,
    \qquad
    R_s^{\mathrm{mid}}:=|\mathcal T-c_s\mathrm{Id}|^{-1/2}.
\]
Since $c_s$ lies in a spectral gap and $c_s>0$, $R_s^{\mathrm{mid}}$ is a bounded
population spectral transform for every $s$. For $j\le s$,
$\lambda_j-c_s\ge(\lambda_j-\lambda_{s+1})/2$; for $k>s$,
$c_s-\lambda_k\ge(\lambda_s-\lambda_k)/2$. Hence
\[
\begin{split}
    b(R_s^{\mathrm{mid}})
    &=\sum_{j\ge1}\frac{\lambda_j}{|\lambda_j-c_s|}\\
    &\le2\left\{
        \sum_{j\le s}\frac{\lambda_j}{\lambda_j-\lambda_{s+1}}
        +\sum_{k>s}\frac{\lambda_k}{\lambda_s-\lambda_k}
       \right\}
     =2\mathfrak r_s^\partial.
\end{split}
\]
The centered covariance bound in Lemma~\ref{lem:spectral-transform-moments}
and Markov's inequality give, with
$\epsilon_{s,N}:=\|R_s^{\mathrm{mid}}\Delta_N R_s^{\mathrm{mid}}\|_\mathrm{op}$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:midpoint-relative-good-event}
    \mathcal G_{s,N}^{\mathrm{mid}}:=\{\epsilon_{s,N}\le1/4\},
    \qquad
    \mathbb P\{(\mathcal G_{s,N}^{\mathrm{mid}})^c\}
    \le\frac{C(\mathfrak r_s^\partial)^2}{N}=o(1).
\end{equation}
The final limit follows from Lemma~\ref{lem:tuning-consequences}.

On $\mathcal G_{s,N}^{\mathrm{mid}}$, for every $h$,
\[
    |\langle h,\Delta_Nh\rangle|
    \le\epsilon_{s,N}
        \langle h,|\mathcal T-c_s\mathrm{Id}|h\rangle.
\]
On the population leading subspace the absolute-value operator is
$\mathcal T-c_s\mathrm{Id}$; on its orthogonal complement it is
$c_s\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal T$. Since $1-\epsilon_{s,N}>0$, the min--max
principle, applied to these two fixed subspaces, yields
\[
\begin{aligned}
    \widehat\lambda_s
    &\ge(1-\epsilon_{s,N})\lambda_s+\epsilon_{s,N}c_s
      =\lambda_s-\epsilon_{s,N}g_s/2,\\
    \widehat\lambda_{s+1}
    &\le(1-\epsilon_{s,N})\lambda_{s+1}+\epsilon_{s,N}c_s
      =\lambda_{s+1}+\epsilon_{s,N}g_s/2.
\end{aligned}
\]
Consequently,
$\widehat\lambda_s-\widehat\lambda_{s+1}
 \ge(1-\epsilon_{s,N})g_s>0$ and
$\widehat\lambda_{s+1}-\mu_s\le g_s/8$ on this event. This proves
$\mathcal G_{s,N}^{\mathrm{mid}}\subseteq\mathcal E_s$ and
\eqref{eq:centered-pca-eigenvalue-good-event}.

For the weighted event, the diagonal operator
\[
    D_s:=|\mathcal T-c_s\mathrm{Id}|^{1/2}S_s
\]
satisfies
\[
    \|D_s\|_\mathrm{op}^2
    =\max_{j\le s}\frac{\lambda_j-c_s}{\lambda_j-\mu_s}\le1.
\]
Because $S_s=R_s^{\mathrm{mid}}D_s=D_s R_s^{\mathrm{mid}}$,
\[
    \|S_s\Delta_NS_s\|_\mathrm{op}
    =\|D_s(R_s^{\mathrm{mid}}\Delta_N R_s^{\mathrm{mid}})D_s\|_\mathrm{op}
    \le\epsilon_{s,N}\le1/4
\]
on $\mathcal G_{s,N}^{\mathrm{mid}}$. Equation~
\eqref{eq:midpoint-relative-good-event} therefore also proves
\eqref{eq:centered-pca-weighted-good-event}. In particular, all the
required good events hold together with probability tending to one,
without an exponential-tail assumption.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem:sharp-leverage-stability}
Under Conditions~\nameref{par:K2}, \nameref{par:K4}--\nameref{par:K5}, and
\eqref{eq:sharp-tuning-regime}, with
$\mathbf B_s=\widehat{\boldsymbol\Lambda}_s+\xi \mathbf I_s$,
\[
    \left\|\mathbf B_s^{-1/2}\overline{\mathbf C}_s\mathbf B_s^{-1/2}\right\|_\mathrm{op}=O_p(1).
\]
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
On $\mathcal E_s^c$, $\overline{\mathbf C}_s=0$ by the proof convention. Put
$\mu=\lambda_{s+1}$. By Lemmas~\ref{lem:derived-covariance-stability}--\ref{lem:empirical-cutoff-separation},
the event
\[
 \mathcal E_s\cap
 \{\delta_{s,N}\le1/4,\ \nu_{s,N}\le1,\
   \widehat\lambda_s\ge\lambda_s/2\}
\]
has probability tending to one. Work on this event. For
$h=\widehat E_s\mathbf a$, \eqref{eq:derived-centered-preconditioned-stability}
yields
\[
 (1-\delta_{s,N})\langle h,\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}h\rangle
 \le \mathbf a^\top\widehat{\boldsymbol\Lambda}_s\mathbf a+\delta_{s,N}\mu\|\mathbf a\|^2.
\]
Here $\mu\|\mathbf a\|^2\le2\mathbf a^\top\widehat{\boldsymbol\Lambda}_s\mathbf a$, so
\[
 \langle h,(\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}+\mu\mathrm{Id})h\rangle
 \le C \mathbf a^\top\widehat{\boldsymbol\Lambda}_s\mathbf a.
\]
Cauchy--Schwarz in regularized coordinates and
\eqref{eq:derived-regularized-mean-stability} then give
\[
 |\langle h,\overline K_N^0\rangle|^2
 \le\nu_{s,N}
     \langle h,(\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}+\mu\mathrm{Id})h\rangle
 \le C\nu_{s,N}\mathbf a^\top\widehat{\boldsymbol\Lambda}_s\mathbf a.
\]
Combining these inequalities with \eqref{eq:supp-overline-C} proves
$\overline{\mathbf C}_s\preceq C\widehat{\boldsymbol\Lambda}_s$ on the displayed event. Hence
\[
 \mathbf B_s^{-1/2}\overline{\mathbf C}_s\mathbf B_s^{-1/2}
 \preceq C \mathbf B_s^{-1/2}\widehat{\boldsymbol\Lambda}_s\mathbf B_s^{-1/2}
 \preceq C\mathbf I_s,
\]
which proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:conditional-moment-lifting}
Assume Condition~\nameref{par:K1}. Let
$\mathbf B\in\mathbb R^{s\times s}$ be positive semidefinite and
$\mathscr X_{N,L}$-measurable.  Conditionally on $\mathscr X_{N,L}$,
\[
    \left\|
      \widehat{\boldsymbol\Phi}_{s}(X)^\top
      \mathbf B\widehat{\boldsymbol\Phi}_{s}(X)
    \right\|_{L^1(\mathbb P_X)}
    =
    \operatorname{tr}(\mathbf B\overline{\mathbf C}_{s}).
\]
For every $\mathscr X_{N,L}$-measurable Hilbert--Schmidt operator $A$,
\begin{equation}
    \left\|\|AK_X^0\|\right\|_{L^2(\mathbb P_X)}
    =\{\operatorname{tr}(A\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}A^*)\}^{1/2},
    \label{eq:conditional-operator-moment-lifting-population}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
For every $h\in\mathcal H_{\kappa_0}$, the covariance identity gives
\[
    \|\langle h,K_X^0\rangle\|_{L^2}
    =
    \langle h,\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}h\rangle^{1/2}.
\]
The quadratic identity follows by taking the conditional expectation of
\(\widehat{\boldsymbol\Phi}_{s}(X)^\top
\mathbf B\widehat{\boldsymbol\Phi}_{s}(X)\).  For the operator identity, diagonalize the
positive trace-class operator \(A^*A=\sum_\ell \mu_\ell u_\ell\otimes u_\ell\).
Then
\[
    \mathbb E\|AK_X^0\|^2
    =
    \sum_\ell\mu_\ell
    \mathbb E|\langle u_\ell,K_X^0\rangle|^2
    =
    \operatorname{tr}(A\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}A^*),
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:conditional-operator-moment-lifting-population}.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lem:deterministic-leading-pca-excess}
Write $\mathcal T=\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}$,
$\Delta_N=\mathcal T-\widehat{\mathcal T}_{\kappa_0,N}$,
$\mu=\lambda_{s+1}$, and $g_s=\lambda_s-\lambda_{s+1}>0$.
On $\mathcal E_s$, suppose that
\[
    \widehat\lambda_{s+1}\le\mu+g_s/2,
    \qquad
    \|S_s\Delta_NS_s\|_\mathrm{op}\le1/4,
    \qquad
    S_s=\sum_{j\le s}(\lambda_j-\mu)^{-1/2}\Pi_j.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:centered-pca-deterministic-leading-bound}
    \sum_{j\le s}(\lambda_j-\mu)
       \|\Pi_j\widehat\Pi_{\le s}^{\perp}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
    \le16\sum_{j\le s}
       \frac{\|\Pi_j\Delta_N\Pi_{>s}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2}{\lambda_j-\mu}.
\end{equation}
The bound holds whenever the displayed conditions are met; no tail or moment
condition is required.
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
The bound is the $d=r=s$, $\mu=\lambda_{s+1}$ specialization of
\citet[arXiv version~3, equation~(3.7)]{reiss2016pca}.
We give a direct proof to separate
the operator inequality from the probabilistic control of the good events.
Let $P=\Pi_{\le s}$, $Q=\Pi_{>s}$, and
\[
    A=(\mathcal T-\mu\mathrm{Id})|_{\operatorname{ran}(P)}.
\]
Let $I_P$ denote the identity on $\operatorname{ran}(P)$. The operator $A$
on this subspace is positive definite with $A\succeq g_s I_P$, and
$S_s=A^{-1/2}P$, extended by zero on $\operatorname{ran}(Q)$. Choose an orthonormal basis
$(v_\ell)_\ell$ of $\operatorname{ran}(\widehat\Pi_{\le s}^{\perp})$
consisting of eigenvectors of the empirical covariance, with eigenvalues
$\nu_\ell\le\widehat\lambda_{s+1}$. This includes an orthonormal basis of
its null space; the empirical covariance is finite rank.
Put $u_\ell=A^{1/2}Pv_\ell$. Projecting
$(\mathcal T-\Delta_N)v_\ell=\nu_\ell v_\ell$ onto
$\operatorname{ran}(P)$ and multiplying by $A^{-1/2}$ gives
\[
    \{I_P+(\mu-\nu_\ell)A^{-1}
          -S_s\Delta_NS_s|_{\operatorname{ran}(P)}\}u_\ell
    =S_s\Delta_NQv_\ell.
\]
If $\nu_\ell\le\mu$, the sum of the first two terms is at least $I_P$.
Otherwise $0<\nu_\ell-\mu\le g_s/2$ and $A^{-1}\preceq g_s^{-1}I_P$,
so that sum is at least $I_P/2$. In either case the operator in braces is
at least $I_P/4$. Its inverse therefore has operator norm at most $4$, and
\[
    \|u_\ell\|\le4\|S_s\Delta_NQv_\ell\|.
\]
Summing the squares over this orthonormal basis gives
\[
\begin{split}
    \sum_{j\le s}(\lambda_j-\mu)
       \|\Pi_j\widehat\Pi_{\le s}^{\perp}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
    &=\sum_\ell\|u_\ell\|^2\\
    &\le16\|S_s\Delta_NQ\widehat\Pi_{\le s}^{\perp}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2\\
    &\le16\|S_s\Delta_NQ\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
     =16\sum_{j\le s}
       \frac{\|\Pi_j\Delta_N\Pi_{>s}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2}{\lambda_j-\mu}.
\end{split}
\]
All these Hilbert--Schmidt norms are finite because $S_s$ is finite rank.
This proves the claim, including when the empirical covariance has a
nontrivial null space.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:relative-pca-projector-control}
Define the leading-side PCA excess
\[
    \mathcal E_{s,N}^{\mathrm{PCA},+}
    :=
    \sum_{j\le s}(\lambda_j-\lambda_{s+1})
    \|\Pi_j\widehat\Pi_{\le s}^\perp\|_\mathrm{HS}^2.
\]
Under Conditions~\nameref{par:K2}, \nameref{par:K4}--\nameref{par:K5}, and the
relative-spectral tuning regime \eqref{eq:sharp-tuning-regime},
\begin{align}
    \|\widehat\Pi_{\le s}-\Pi_{\le s}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
    &=O_p\!\left(\frac{\mathcal U_s\log N}{N}\right)
     =O_p\!\left(\frac{s^2\log(es)\log N}{N}\right),
    \label{eq:relative-pca-unweighted-bound}\\
    \mathcal E_{s,N}^{\mathrm{PCA},+}
    &=O_p\!\left(\frac{\mathcal W_s^{+}}{N}\right)
     =O_p\!\left(\frac{s^{2-q}\log(es)}{N}\right).
    \label{eq:relative-pca-leading-excess-bound}
\end{align}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:relative-pca-prediction-weighted-bound}
    \|\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}^{1/2}(\widehat\Pi_{\le s}-\Pi_{\le s})\|_\mathrm{HS}
    =
    O_p\!\left(
      \sqrt{\frac{s^{2-q}\log(es)\log N}{N}}
    \right).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Put $Z=K_X^0$, $\mathcal T=\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}$, and
\[
 \widetilde{\mathcal T}_N:=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^N Z_i\otimes Z_i,
 \qquad
 \bar Z_N:=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^N Z_i.
\]
Thus the perturbation generating the projector used in the estimator is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:centered-relative-perturbation}
 \widehat{\mathcal T}_{\kappa_0,N}-\mathcal T
 =(\widetilde{\mathcal T}_N-\mathcal T_{\kappa_0})
  -\bar Z_N\otimes\bar Z_N.
\end{equation}
Recall $e_j=\sqrt{\lambda_j}\phi_j$. The standardized
Karhunen--Lo\`eve coordinate of $Z$ in direction $e_j$ is
\[
    \eta_j(X)
    :=\lambda_j^{-1/2}\langle e_j,Z\rangle
    =\phi_j(X).
\]
Condition~\nameref{par:K4}, in the fixed population eigenbasis, gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:relative-pca-eighth-moment}
    \sup_{j\ge1}\mathbb E|\eta_j|^8\le C_{X,8}<\infty.
\end{equation}
Thus the coordinate-moment assumption in
\citet[Lemma~1]{jirak2020perturbation} holds with moment order $8$.
Independence of different coordinates is not needed.

We next verify the finite-sample event required by the block version of the
relative perturbation theorem of
\citet{jirak2020perturbation,jirak2023relative}. To make the terminology
explicit, recall the population spectral decomposition
\[
    \mathcal T=\sum_{j\ge1}\lambda_j\Pi_j,
    \qquad \Pi_j=e_j\otimes e_j.
\]
A \emph{spectral block} is an index set \(B\subset\mathbb N\) representing
the population invariant subspace
\(\operatorname{ran}(\Pi_B)\), where
\[
    \Pi_B:=\sum_{j\in B}\Pi_j,
    \qquad
    b_B:=\operatorname{tr}(\Pi_B\mathcal T)=\sum_{j\in B}\lambda_j.
\]
Thus \(\Pi_B(\widehat{\mathcal T}_{\kappa_0,N}-\mathcal T)\Pi_C\) is the \((B,C)\) rectangular
submatrix of the perturbation when the operator is written in the population
eigenbasis; explicitly,
\[
 \Pi_B(\widehat{\mathcal T}_{\kappa_0,N}-\mathcal T)\Pi_C
 =\sum_{j\in B}\sum_{k\in C}
   \langle e_j,(\widehat{\mathcal T}_{\kappa_0,N}-\mathcal T)e_k\rangle
   e_j\otimes e_k.
\]
Here ``block'' refers to a group of population eigendirections, not to a
block of observations.

The target projector is indexed by
\[
    I:=\{1,\ldots,s\}.
\]
Thus $\Pi_I=\Pi_{\le s}$.
The set \(I\) is generally a union of spectral blocks rather than a single
block. Condition~\nameref{par:K2} ensures that no population eigenspace is
split at its boundary because \(\lambda_s>\lambda_{s+1}\). To construct the
comparison partition, let
\[
 k_0:=\bigwedge\{k>s:\lambda_k\le\lambda_s/2\}.
\]
Condition~\nameref{par:K5} implies $k_0\asymp s$. In the notation of the
cited block theorem, set
\[
 I':=\{1,\ldots,k_0-1\}.
\]
For every distinct eigenvalue \(\mu\) among
\(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_{k_0-1}\), define
\[
    B(\mu):=\{j<k_0:\lambda_j=\mu\},
    \qquad
    B_{\mathrm{tail}}:=\{k_0,k_0+1,\ldots\}.
\]
The nonempty sets \(B(\mu)\), together with \(B_{\mathrm{tail}}\), form the
comparison partition. In particular, repeated eigenvalues remain in the same
block, while all sufficiently small eigenvalues are collected in the single
tail block.

\@startsection{paragraph}{4}{\z@}{1.5ex plus
   0.5ex minus .2ex}{-1em}{\normalsize\bfseries}{Specialized block perturbation principle.}
The form of \citet[Theorem~2]{jirak2020perturbation} used below can be read as
follows. Suppose the perturbation between every pair of blocks $B,C$ satisfies
\[
 \|\Pi_B(\widehat{\mathcal T}_{\kappa_0,N}-\mathcal T)\Pi_C\|_\mathrm{HS}
 \le x\sqrt{b_Bb_C},
\]
the tail is separated from the target directions, and
$x\mathfrak r_s^\partial\le1/8$. Then, on the event that the empirical leading
index set corresponds to $I=\{1,\ldots,s\}$, the theorem gives
\[
 \|\widehat\Pi_{\le s}-\Pi_{\le s}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
 \le Cx^2
 \sum_{j\le s}\sum_{k>s}
 \frac{\lambda_j\lambda_k}{(\lambda_j-\lambda_k)^2}
 =Cx^2\mathcal U_s.
\]
Thus the remainder of this part verifies the three hypotheses in the present
notation; it does not introduce a new perturbation argument.

For this partition, the three inputs to be verified are the following:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item for every pair of blocks \(B,C\), the relative block bound
\[
 \left\|\Pi_B\{(\widetilde{\mathcal T}_N-\mathcal T_{\kappa_0})
       -\bar Z_N\otimes\bar Z_N\}\Pi_C\right\|_\mathrm{HS}
 \le x_N\sqrt{b_Bb_C};
\]
\item for every \(i\in I\) and \(k\notin I'\), the far-block separation
\[
    |\lambda_i-\lambda_k|\ge\lambda_i/2;
\]
\item the contraction condition
\[
    x_N\mathfrak r_s^\partial\le\frac18
\]
for all sufficiently large \(N\), where the relative coefficient \(x_N\) is
specified below.
\end{enumerate}

The far-block condition is immediate. For \(i\in I\) and \(k\notin I'\),
the definition of \(k_0\) gives
\[
 \lambda_k\le\lambda_s/2\le\lambda_i/2,
 \qquad
 |\lambda_i-\lambda_k|\ge\lambda_i/2.
\]
It remains to establish the relative block bound and the contraction
condition. For this purpose, let \(B,C\) be any two blocks in the displayed
partition. Then \citet[Lemma~1]{jirak2020perturbation}, applied to each block pair
and followed by a union bound, gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:relative-pca-uncentered-block-event}
 \|\Pi_B(\widetilde{\mathcal T}_N-\mathcal T_{\kappa_0})\Pi_C\|_\mathrm{HS}
 \le C\frac{t}{\sqrt N}\sqrt{b_Bb_C}.
\end{equation}
For $t\ge1$ the exceptional probability is at most
\[
 Ck_0^2\{N^{-1}t^{-4}+e^{-t^2}\}.
\]
This records explicitly the moment order, the far-block index, and the
failure probability of the block event used below.

Empirical centering is controlled on the same partition by fourth moments.
For every block $B$, including the infinite tail block,
Lemma~\ref{lem:spectral-transform-moments} applied to $\Pi_B$ gives
\[
    \mathbb E\|\Pi_B\bar Z_N\|^4\le\frac{Cb_B^2}{N^2}.
\]
All blocks are nonempty and have $b_B>0$. For $N\ge3$, put
\[
    u_N:=\sqrt{\frac{\log N}{N}},\qquad
    \mathcal C_N:=\bigcap_B
       \{\|\Pi_B\bar Z_N\|^2\le b_Bu_N\}.
\]
There are at most $k_0+1$ blocks, so Markov's inequality and a union bound
yield
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:relative-pca-centering-failure}
    \mathbb P(\mathcal C_N^c)
    \le\frac{C(k_0+1)}{N^2u_N^2}
    =\frac{C(k_0+1)}{N\log N}.
\end{equation}
On $\mathcal C_N$, simultaneously for every pair of blocks,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:relative-pca-centering-block-bound}
    \|\Pi_B(\bar Z_N\otimes\bar Z_N)\Pi_C\|_\mathrm{HS}
    =\|\Pi_B\bar Z_N\|\,\|\Pi_C\bar Z_N\|
    \le u_N\sqrt{b_Bb_C}.
\end{equation}
Only the relative scale $u_N$ is needed by the block theorem; no
exponential-confidence bound for the sample mean is used.

Take $t_N=\sqrt{4\log N}$ in
\eqref{eq:relative-pca-uncentered-block-event}. On its intersection with
$\mathcal C_N$, the combined perturbation satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:relative-pca-combined-block-event}
    \|\Pi_B\{(\widetilde{\mathcal T}_N-\mathcal T_{\kappa_0})
           -\bar Z_N\otimes\bar Z_N\}\Pi_C\|_\mathrm{HS}
    \le x_N\sqrt{b_Bb_C}
    \quad\text{for every }B,C,
\end{equation}
where $x_N=C\sqrt{\log N/N}$, with a sufficiently large fixed
constant depending only on $C_{X,8}$. Let $\mathcal B_N$ denote the
simultaneous event in \eqref{eq:relative-pca-combined-block-event}.
The two union bounds give
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:relative-pca-block-failure}
\begin{split}
    \mathbb P(\mathcal B_N^c)
    &\le Ck_0^2\{N^{-1}t_N^{-4}+e^{-t_N^2}\}
          +\frac{C(k_0+1)}{N\log N}\\
    &\le C\left\{
          \frac{s^2}{N(\log N)^2}
          +\frac{s^2}{N^4}
          +\frac{s+1}{N\log N}\right\}
     =o(1).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Here $k_0\asymp s$, the first term tends to zero by
Lemma~\ref{lem:tuning-consequences}, and the remaining terms vanish because
$s<N$ and $N\to\infty$.
For the selected leading index set $I=\{1,\ldots,s\}$, the separation
quantity in the cited block theorem is $\mathfrak r_s^\partial$. Lemmas~
\ref{lem:cross-boundary-spectrum-consequences} and
\ref{lem:tuning-consequences} give
\[
 x_N\mathfrak r_s^\partial=o(1).
\]
In particular, \(x_N\mathfrak r_s^\partial\le1/8\) for all sufficiently
large \(N\), as required by the block perturbation theorem. The relative
block bounds above apply simultaneously to every pair of blocks in the
near/tail partition.
The block failure bounds above and Lemma~
\ref{lem:empirical-cutoff-separation} show that the intersection of
$\mathcal E_s$ with all the block events has probability tending to one. On
this intersection, all inputs required by the relative
perturbation theorem are present: the cross-block inequalities are
\eqref{eq:relative-pca-combined-block-event}, the far-block separation follows
from the definition of \(k_0\), and
\(x_N\mathfrak r_s^\partial\le1/8\) gives the contraction condition.
Therefore \citet[Theorem~2]{jirak2020perturbation} applies to the displayed
block partition and to the combined perturbation
