$(\widetilde{\mathcal T}_N-\mathcal T_{\kappa_0})-\bar Z_N\otimes\bar Z_N$. With
$I=\{1,\ldots,s\}$, the event \(\mathcal E_s\) identifies the empirical
projector indexed by \(I\) with \(\widehat\Pi_{\le s}\), and the theorem gives the
bound
\[
    \|\widehat\Pi_{\le s}-\Pi_{\le s}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
    \le Cx_N^2\mathcal U_s.
\]
Since $x_N^2=O(\log N/N)$, Lemma~
\ref{lem:cross-boundary-spectrum-consequences} proves
\eqref{eq:relative-pca-unweighted-bound}. Notice that this argument retains
the logarithmic confidence factor throughout.

We next prove the one-sided excess using
Lemma~\ref{lem:deterministic-leading-pca-excess}. Put
\[
    \mu_s:=\lambda_{s+1},\qquad
    \Delta_N:=\mathcal T-\widehat{\mathcal T}_{\kappa_0,N}
       =-(\widetilde{\mathcal T}_N-\mathcal T_{\kappa_0})
          +\bar Z_N\otimes\bar Z_N.
\]
Work on $\mathcal G_{s,N}^{\mathrm{mid}}$ from
\eqref{eq:midpoint-relative-good-event}. The proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:empirical-cutoff-separation} shows that this event is
contained in $\mathcal E_s$ and satisfies both hypotheses of the
excess lemma. Hence
\eqref{eq:centered-pca-deterministic-leading-bound} applies on an event
whose probability tends to one. No covariance-relative sub-Gaussian
assumption is used in this step.
It remains to control the right-hand side. Since the population covariance
has zero cross-blocks and the observations are independent,
\[
 \mathbb E\|\Pi_j(\widetilde{\mathcal T}_N-\mathcal T_{\kappa_0})\Pi_{>s}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
 =\frac1N\mathbb E[\|\Pi_jZ\|^2\|\Pi_{>s}Z\|^2]
 \le\frac{C\lambda_j}{N}\sum_{k>s}\lambda_k.
\]
Indeed,
\[
 \|\Pi_jZ\|^2=\lambda_j\eta_j^2,
 \qquad
 \|\Pi_{>s}Z\|^2=\sum_{k>s}\lambda_k\eta_k^2,
\]
so Tonelli's theorem, Cauchy--Schwarz, and
\eqref{eq:relative-pca-eighth-moment} give
\[
 \mathbb E[\|\Pi_jZ\|^2\|\Pi_{>s}Z\|^2]
 =\lambda_j\sum_{k>s}\lambda_k\mathbb E(\eta_j^2\eta_k^2)
 \le C\lambda_j\sum_{k>s}\lambda_k.
\]
Hence
\[
 \sum_{j\le s}
 \frac{\|\Pi_j(\widetilde{\mathcal T}_N-\mathcal T_{\kappa_0})\Pi_{>s}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2}
      {\lambda_j-\mu_s}
 =O_p(\mathcal W_s^+/N).
\]
For the rank-one centering term, put
$U_j=\langle e_j,\bar Z_N\rangle$ and
$\bar\eta_j=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^N\eta_j(X_i)$, so
$U_j=\sqrt{\lambda_j}\bar\eta_j$. Since the summands are independent and
centered, expansion of the fourth power and
\eqref{eq:relative-pca-eighth-moment} give
\[
 \mathbb E\bar\eta_j^4
 =N^{-4}\left\{N\mathbb E\eta_j^4
   +3N(N-1)(\mathbb E\eta_j^2)^2\right\}
 \le C/N^2.
\]
Cauchy--Schwarz therefore yields
\[
 \mathbb E(U_j^2U_k^2)
 \le\lambda_j\lambda_k
   (\mathbb E\bar\eta_j^4\,\mathbb E\bar\eta_k^4)^{1/2}
 \le C\lambda_j\lambda_k/N^2.
\]
Moreover,
\[
 \|\Pi_j(\bar Z_N\otimes\bar Z_N)\Pi_{>s}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
 =U_j^2\sum_{k>s}U_k^2.
\]
By this identity and Tonelli's theorem,
\[
 \mathbb E\sum_{j\le s}
 \frac{\|\Pi_j(\bar Z_N\otimes\bar Z_N)\Pi_{>s}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2}
      {\lambda_j-\mu_s}
 \le
 \frac{C}{N^2}
 \left(\sum_{k>s}\lambda_k\right)
 \left(\sum_{j\le s}\frac{\lambda_j}{\lambda_j-\mu_s}\right)
 =\frac{C\mathcal W_s^+}{N^2}.
\]
By the elementary inequality
\[
 \|\Pi_j\Delta_N\Pi_{>s}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
 \le2\|\Pi_j(\widetilde{\mathcal T}_N-\mathcal T_{\kappa_0})\Pi_{>s}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
    +2\|\Pi_j(\bar Z_N\otimes\bar Z_N)\Pi_{>s}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2,
\]
the nonnegative quantity
\[
    V_{s,N}:=\sum_{j\le s}
       \frac{\|\Pi_j\Delta_N\Pi_{>s}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2}{\lambda_j-\mu_s}
\]
satisfies $\mathbb E V_{s,N}\le C\mathcal W_s^+/N$.
For every $M>0$, the excess lemma and Markov's inequality
therefore give
\[
    \mathbb P\!\left(\mathcal E_{s,N}^{\mathrm{PCA},+}
                >M\mathcal W_s^+/N\right)
    \le\mathbb P\{(\mathcal G_{s,N}^{\mathrm{mid}})^c\}
       +\mathbb P\!\left(16V_{s,N}>M\mathcal W_s^+/N\right)
    \le o(1)+\frac{C}{M}.
\]
Thus $\mathcal E_{s,N}^{\mathrm{PCA},+}=O_p(\mathcal W_s^+/N)$.
No independence between the good event and $V_{s,N}$ is required.
Lemma~\ref{lem:cross-boundary-spectrum-consequences} now gives
\eqref{eq:relative-pca-leading-excess-bound}.

It remains to convert these two PCA quantities into the prediction-weighted
norm through an operator identity. On $\mathcal E_s$, put
\[
    a_j:=\|\widehat\Pi_{\le s}^\perp e_j\|^2\quad(j\le s),
    \qquad
    b_k:=\|\widehat\Pi_{\le s} e_k\|^2\quad(k>s).
\]
Since $\Pi_{\le s}$ and $\widehat\Pi_{\le s}$ are orthogonal projectors of the same finite
rank,
\[
    \sum_{j\le s}a_j
    =\sum_{k>s}b_k
    =\frac12\|\widehat\Pi_{\le s}-\Pi_{\le s}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2.
\]
Moreover,
\begin{align*}
 \|\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}^{1/2}(\widehat\Pi_{\le s}-\Pi_{\le s})\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
 &=
 \sum_{j\le s}\lambda_j a_j+\sum_{k>s}\lambda_k b_k\\
 &\le
 \sum_{j\le s}(\lambda_j-\lambda_{s+1})a_j
 +\lambda_{s+1}\left(\sum_{j\le s}a_j+\sum_{k>s}b_k\right)\\
 &=
 \mathcal E_{s,N}^{\mathrm{PCA},+}
 +\lambda_{s+1}\|\widehat\Pi_{\le s}-\Pi_{\le s}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2.
\end{align*}
Using $\lambda_{s+1}\asymp s^{-q}$ together with
\eqref{eq:relative-pca-unweighted-bound} and
\eqref{eq:relative-pca-leading-excess-bound} proves
\eqref{eq:relative-pca-prediction-weighted-bound}.  Lemma~
\ref{lem:empirical-cutoff-separation} gives
$\mathbb P(\mathcal E_s^c)\to0$, and the exceptional-event convention preserves
the $O_p$ conclusion. These are probability-order bounds under the
eighth-moment condition; the proof does not assert exponential failure
probabilities or unconditional mean bounds for the empirical projector.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- proposition-8: body_md -->
\label{prop:sharp-spectral-verification}
Under Conditions~\nameref{par:K1}--\nameref{par:K2}, \nameref{par:K4}, and
\nameref{par:K5}, and under the relative-spectral tuning regime
\eqref{eq:sharp-tuning-regime}, the full-sample empirical eigensystem
constructed in Section~\ref{app:empirical-spectral-construction} satisfies
the following spectral bounds:
\[
    \frac{\widehat\lambda_s}{\lambda_s}\xrightarrow{p}1,\qquad
    \|\mathbf B_s^{-1/2}\overline{\mathbf C}_{s}\mathbf B_s^{-1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_p(1),
\]
\[
    \|\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}^{1/2}(\widehat\Pi_{\le s}-\Pi_{\le s})\|_{\mathrm{HS}}
    =O_p\!\left(
      \sqrt{\frac{s^{2-q}\log(es)\log N}{N}}
    \right),
\]
\[
    \|\overline K_N^0\|_{\mathcal H_{\kappa_0}}
    =O_p(N^{-1/2}).
\]
<!-- end proposition-8 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Let
\[
    \widetilde{\mathcal T}_N
    :=
    \frac1N\sum_{i=1}^N K_{X_i}^0\otimes K_{X_i}^0.
\]
The full-sample empirical centered covariance operator generated by
Section~\ref{app:empirical-spectral-construction} is
\[
    \widehat{\mathcal T}_{{\kappa_0},N}
    =
    \widetilde{\mathcal T}_N
    -\overline K_N^0
     \otimes\overline K_N^0,
\]
so its leading eigenpairs are exactly
\((\widehat\lambda_j,\widehat e_j)\) on $\mathcal E_s$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:empirical-eigenvalue-comparability} gives
$\widehat\lambda_s/\lambda_s\xrightarrow{p}1$, and
Lemma~\ref{lem:sharp-leverage-stability} gives the ridge-leverage bound.
Equation~\eqref{eq:derived-centering-rate} gives
$\|\overline K_N^0\|=O_p(N^{-1/2})$, while
Lemma~\ref{lem:relative-pca-projector-control} gives
\[
    \|\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}^{1/2}(\widehat\Pi_{\le s}-\Pi_{\le s})\|_\mathrm{HS}
    =O_p\!\left(
      \sqrt{\frac{s^{2-q}\log(es)\log N}{N}}
    \right).
\]
The displayed projector rate converges to zero. For $1<q<2$,
\[
 \frac{s^{2-q}\log(es)\log N}{N}
 \le
 \frac{s^2\log^2(es)\log N}{N}=o(1)
\]
by the first tuning restriction. For $q\ge2$, the squared rate is at most
$\log(es)\log N/N=o(1)$.

Labeled fitting stability follows from Lemma~\ref{lem:labeled-gram-stability},
not by treating its inverse as constant when averaging over $L$.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:labeled-full-sample-design-comparison}
For $f_{n,N}=(N-n)/\{n(N-1)\}$, conditional on all pooled covariates,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:labeled-full-sample-design-comparison}
\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_L[\overline{\boldsymbol\Phi}_L\overline{\boldsymbol\Phi}_L^\top]
 &=f_{n,N}\widehat{\boldsymbol\Lambda}_s,\\
 \mathbb E_L[\mathbf C_{L,s}]
 &=\frac{N(n-1)}{n(N-1)}\widehat{\boldsymbol\Lambda}_s,\qquad
 \mathbb E_L\|\mathbf B_s^{-1/2}\overline{\boldsymbol\Phi}_L\|^2\le s/n.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
The inclusion probabilities are $n/N$ and $n(n-1)/\{N(N-1)\}$.
Use $P_N\widehat{\boldsymbol\Phi}_s=0$ and
$P_N\widehat{\boldsymbol\Phi}_s\widehat{\boldsymbol\Phi}_s^\top
=\widehat{\boldsymbol\Lambda}_s$. Subtract the mean outer product from
$\mathbb E_L\mathbf C^u_{L,s}=\widehat{\boldsymbol\Lambda}_s$ and whiten.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lem:labeled-gram-stability}
Under either spectral route, put
\[
 \eta_{L,N}:=\|\mathbf B_s^{-1/2}(\mathbf C_{L,s}
                     -\widehat{\boldsymbol\Lambda}_s)\mathbf B_s^{-1/2}\|_\mathrm{op}.
\]
Then $s/n\to0$ and $\eta_{L,N}=o_p(1)$. For any fixed $0<\delta<1$, with probability
tending to one,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:labeled-inverse-sandwich}
 (1-\delta)\mathbf B_s\preceq\mathbf C_{L,s}+\xi I
 \preceq(1+\delta)\mathbf B_s,\qquad
 \|\mathbf B_s^{1/2}\mathbf A_s\mathbf B_s^{1/2}\|_\mathrm{op}
 \le(1-\delta)^{-1}.
\end{equation}
For $N=n$ these hold exactly with $\eta_{L,N}=0$.
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
First, the basic conditions already imply $s/n\to0$. Put
$C_{\rm tr}:=\operatorname{tr}(\mathcal T_{\kappa_0})<\infty$ and
$g_s:=\lambda_s-\lambda_{s+1}$. Since $s\lambda_s\le C_{\rm tr}$
and $\xi/\lambda_s\to0$, along indices with $N>n$ we have eventually
\[
 \frac{s}{n}
 \le\frac{C_{\rm tr}}{n\lambda_s}
 \le\frac{2C_{\rm tr}}{n(\lambda_s+\xi)}\longrightarrow0
\]
by the labeled condition in \eqref{eq:basic-tuning-regime}. Along indices with $N=n$,
$g_s\le\lambda_s\le C_{\rm tr}/s$ gives
\[
 \frac{s^2}{n}\le\frac{C_{\rm tr}^2}{n g_s^2}\longrightarrow0
\]
by the basic eigengap condition, which also implies $s/n\to0$.
For the relative route, $s/n\to0$ follows from its retained assumption
$s^2/n\to0$.

If $N=n$, the centered labeled covariance equals the pooled diagonal
covariance identically, so $\eta_{L,N}=0$ and the sandwich is exact.
It remains to prove the assertions along indices with $N>n$.
For the basic route, condition on the pooled covariates and set
$v_i=\mathbf B_s^{-1/2}\widehat{\boldsymbol\Phi}_s(X_i)$,
$\ell_N=\max(1,\max_i\|v_i\|^2)$ and
$S_N=P_Nvv^\top\preceq I$. The centered finite population
$v_iv_i^\top-S_N$ has norm at most $2\ell_N$ and matrix variance at most
$\ell_N I$. The without-replacement matrix Bernstein bound
\citep[Theorem~1]{gross2010note} gives, with conditional probability
at least $1-\varepsilon$,
\[
 \|P_Lvv^\top-S_N\|_\mathrm{op}
 \le C\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\ell_N\log(2s/\varepsilon)}n}
               +\frac{\ell_N\log(2s/\varepsilon)}n\right\}.
\]
Write $Z_N$ for the left-hand side and $b_N(\varepsilon)$ for the
right-hand side, which is measurable with respect to the pooled covariates.
The zero-extension convention on $\mathcal E_s^c$ gives $Z_N=0$ there,
so the conditional probability bound holds on that event as well.
Bounded kernel and eigenvalue comparability give
$\ell_N\le 1+4B_\kappa/(\widehat\lambda_s+\xi)$.
The labeled condition in \eqref{eq:basic-tuning-regime} gives
$b_N(\varepsilon)=o_p(1)$ for each fixed $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$.
For every fixed $a>0$, integrating the conditional bound yields
\[
 \mathbb P(Z_N>a)
 \le \mathbb P\{Z_N>b_N(\varepsilon)\}
       +\mathbb P\{b_N(\varepsilon)>a\}
 \le \varepsilon+\mathbb P\{b_N(\varepsilon)>a\}.
\]
Thus $\limsup_{n,N\to\infty}\mathbb P(Z_N>a)\le\varepsilon$.
Letting $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ proves $Z_N=o_p(1)$ unconditionally.
Centering subtracts $(P_Lv)(P_Lv)^\top$, whose norm is $O_p(s/n)=o_p(1)$
by Lemma~\ref{lem:labeled-full-sample-design-comparison}.

For the relative route, work directly in the population-centered RKHS.
Put $\mu=\lambda_{s+1}$, $R_\mu=(\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}+\mu I)^{-1/2}$
and $d_\mu=\mathcal N(\mu)\lesssim s$, the last inequality following
from \hyperref[par:K5]{\textnormal{(K5)}}. For $Z=K_X^0$, \hyperref[par:K4]{\textnormal{(K4)}} and Cauchy--Schwarz give
\[
 \mathbb E\|R_\mu Z\|^4
 =\sum_{j,k}\frac{\lambda_j\lambda_k\,\mathbb E[\phi_j^2\phi_k^2]}
                   {(\lambda_j+\mu)(\lambda_k+\mu)}
 \le C d_\mu^2.
\]
For an iid sample of size $m$, let
$\widetilde{\mathcal T}_m=P_mZ\otimes Z$ and
$\widehat{\mathcal T}_m=\widetilde{\mathcal T}_m-\bar Z_m\otimes\bar Z_m$.
Hilbert--Schmidt variance and the mean identity yield
\[
 \mathbb E\|R_\mu(\widetilde{\mathcal T}_m-\mathcal T_{\kappa_0})R_\mu\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
 \le Cd_\mu^2/m,\qquad
 \mathbb E\|R_\mu\bar Z_m\|^2=d_\mu/m.
\]
Consequently
$\delta_m:=\|R_\mu(\widehat{\mathcal T}_m-\mathcal T_{\kappa_0})R_\mu\|_\mathrm{op}
=O_p(s/\sqrt m)$.
Apply this separately to $m=n,N$: the labeled observations are marginally
iid, and no independence between these two samples is required.
The assumed $s^2/n\to0$, with $N\ge n$, makes both discrepancies vanish.

The transfer to empirical coordinates does not presume the conclusion.
On $\delta_N<1/2$,
\[
 (1-\delta_N)(\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}+\mu I)
 \preceq\widehat{\mathcal T}_N+\mu I.
\]
Then $\widehat\lambda_s\ge(1-2\delta_N)\lambda_s$.
So $\mu\le2\widehat\lambda_s$ with probability tending to one.
It follows that
$U_N=(\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}+\mu I)^{1/2}\widehat E_s\mathbf B_s^{-1/2}$
has 
\[
\|U_N\|_{\mathrm{op}}^2
\le \frac{1}{1-\delta_N}
\left(1+\frac{\mu}{\widehat\lambda_s}\right)
=O_p(1).
\]
Labeled centering and the empirical
eigenvector equations give the exact identities
\[
 \mathbf C_{L,s}=\widehat E_s^*\widehat{\mathcal T}_L\widehat E_s,\qquad
 \widehat{\boldsymbol\Lambda}_s
       =\widehat E_s^*\widehat{\mathcal T}_N\widehat E_s.
\]
Thus
\[
 \eta_{L,N}\le\|U_N\|_\mathrm{op}^2(\delta_n+\delta_N)
 =O_p(s/\sqrt n+s/\sqrt N)=o_p(1).
\]
This avoids assuming independence of learned eigenvectors and labeled data.
The sandwich follows by adding $I$ to the whitened discrepancy.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lem:labeled-weight-bound}
Under either spectral route, let
$\mathbf Q_L=\mathbb E_{X^\circ}[\mathbf q_L(X^\circ)
\mathbf q_L(X^\circ)^\top\mid\mathscr X_{N,L}]$. Then
\[
 \|\mathbf B_s^{-1/2}\mathbf Q_L\mathbf B_s^{-1/2}\|_\mathrm{op}=O_p(1),\qquad
 \mathbb E_{X^\circ}\|\mathbf a_{s,\xi}(X^\circ)\|^2=O_p(s/n).
\]
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
Use $\mathbf Q_L\preceq2\overline{\mathbf C}_s+
2\overline{\boldsymbol\Phi}_L\overline{\boldsymbol\Phi}_L^\top$,
the applicable pooled query bound and
\eqref{eq:labeled-full-sample-design-comparison}. Since
$\mathbf A_s\mathbf C_{L,s}\mathbf A_s
=\mathbf A_s-\xi\mathbf A_s^2\preceq\mathbf A_s$,
\[
 \mathbb E_{X^\circ}\|\mathbf a_{s,\xi}(X^\circ)\|^2
 \le n^{-1}+n^{-1}\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf A_s\mathbf Q_L)
 =O_p(s/n)
\]
by \eqref{eq:labeled-inverse-sandwich}. The random inverse is bounded
before averaging, not removed from an expectation over $L$.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lem:sharp-response-noise}
Under either spectral route and \hyperref[par:R2]{\textnormal{(R2)}},
\[
 \left\|\sup_t|\widehat C_{s,\xi}(t;X^\circ)
                 -\overline C^L_{s,\xi}(t;X^\circ)|\right\|_{2,X\mid\mathscr D_{n,N}}
 =O_p(\sqrt{s/n}).
\]
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Conditional on covariates, $L$ and a query, the difference equals
$\sum_{i\in L}a_{s,\xi,i}(X^\circ)\zeta_t(X_i,Y_i)$.
The weights are fixed under this conditioning and do not depend on the responses. Apply \hyperref[par:R2]{\textnormal{(R2)}},
square and integrate over the independent query. The conditional expected
squared norm is bounded by
$C_M^2\mathbb E_{X^\circ}\|\mathbf a_{s,\xi}(X^\circ)\|^2=O_p(s/n)$.
Conditional Markov's inequality on events where this bound is at most
$Cs/n$ proves the assertion. No inverse moments on exceptional events
are required for this probability-order conclusion.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{lem:hilbert-smoother-stability}
Under either spectral route, let $\mathcal V$ be a separable Hilbert space.
There is a nonnegative $\mathscr X_{N,L}$-measurable random constant
$C_{n,N}=O_p(1)$ such that, simultaneously for all inputs
$(V_i)_{i\in L}\in\mathcal V^n$,
\[
 \left\|\sum_{i\in L}a_{s,\xi,i}(\cdot)V_i
       \right\|_{L^2(\mathbb P_X;\mathcal V)}^2
 \le C_{n,N}\,\frac1n\sum_{i\in L}\|V_i\|_{\mathcal V}^2.
\]
The inputs may depend on the pooled covariates and on $L$. No independence
between the inputs and the weights is required for this inequality.
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
Put $\mathbf D_L=\mathbf C_{L,s}+\xi\mathbf I_s$ and retain
$\mathbf Q_L=\mathbb E_{X^\circ}[\mathbf q_L(X^\circ)\mathbf q_L(X^\circ)^\top
\mid\mathscr X_{N,L}]$. Lemmas~\ref{lem:labeled-gram-stability} and
\ref{lem:labeled-weight-bound} imply
\[
 c_{n,N}:=\|\mathbf D_L^{-1/2}\mathbf Q_L\mathbf D_L^{-1/2}\|_\mathrm{op}=O_p(1).
\]
For the fixed input values set $\bar V_L=n^{-1}\sum_i V_i$ and define
$\widehat B:\mathbb R^s\to\mathcal V$ by
\[
 \widehat B\mathbf D_L
   =\frac1n\sum_{i\in L}(V_i-\bar V_L)\otimes\mathbf q_L(X_i).
\]
This is the unique slope in the Hilbert-valued centered ridge fit, and
its fitted function is $\bar V_L+\widehat B\mathbf q_L(x)$.
Expansion of its penalized least-squares objective at the optimum gives
\[
 0\le \frac1n\sum_i\|V_i-\bar V_L\|^2
       -\|\widehat B\mathbf D_L^{1/2}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2.
\]
Since $n^{-1}\sum_i\|V_i\|^2=\|\bar V_L\|^2+
 n^{-1}\sum_i\|V_i-\bar V_L\|^2$, it follows that
\[
 \|\bar V_L\|^2+\|\widehat B\mathbf D_L^{1/2}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
 \le\frac1n\sum_i\|V_i\|^2.
\]
Query integration and $\mathbf Q_L\preceq c_{n,N}\mathbf D_L$ yield
\[
\begin{split}
 \|\bar V_L+\widehat B\mathbf q_L\|_{L^2}^2
 &\le2\|\bar V_L\|^2+2\|\widehat B\mathbf Q_L^{1/2}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2\\
 &\le2\max\{1,c_{n,N}\}
       \big(\|\bar V_L\|^2+\|\widehat B\mathbf D_L^{1/2}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2\big).
\end{split}
\]
Take $C_{n,N}=2\max\{1,c_{n,N}\}$. This constant does not depend on
$\mathcal V$ or the input values. All inequalities are deterministic
conditional on the design, which proves the simultaneous assertion.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{lem:approximation-residual-bound}
Under either spectral route and \hyperref[par:M1]{\textnormal{(M1)}}, \hyperref[par:M2]{\textnormal{(M2)}}, and \hyperref[par:R1]{\textnormal{(R1)}},
\[
 \left\|\sup_t|(\widehat S-I)r_{t,s}|\right\|_{2,X\mid\mathscr X_{N,L}}
 =O_p(b_{r,s}).
\]
Under the Hilbert-source assumptions of
Proposition~\ref{prop:hilbert-automatic-complexity}, the right side can
be replaced by $O_p(b^{\mathrm H}_{r,s})$.
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
Use the common $\ell^2$ representation in
Lemma~\ref{lem:automatic-spectral-truncation}. Linearity gives
\[
 \sup_t|(\widehat S-I)r_{t,s}(x)|
 \le B_r\|(\widehat S-I)v_{r,s}(x)\|_{\ell^2}.
\]
The vector $v_{r,s}$ is deterministic because the population kernel,
its eigenfunctions, and $s$ are specified without observing the data.
The labeled covariates are marginally iid, so
\[
 \mathbb E P_L\|v_{r,s}\|^2=\|v_{r,s}\|_{L^2}^2=\sum_{j>s}\lambda_j^r.
\]
Markov's inequality and Lemma~\ref{lem:hilbert-smoother-stability} give
$\|\widehat S v_{r,s}\|_{L^2}=O_p(\|v_{r,s}\|_{L^2})$. The triangle
inequality proves the result. No conditional-mean-zero assumption is
made on this residual. For Hilbert responses use the deterministic
vector tail $\sum_{j>s}\theta_j\phi_j$ instead; its squared $L^2$ norm
is $\sum_{j>s}\|\theta_j\|^2$, as established in
Proposition~\ref{prop:hilbert-automatic-complexity}.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label{lem:sharp-subspace-perturbation}
Under either spectral route and \hyperref[par:R1]{\textnormal{(R1)}}, let $e_{N,s}$ be the
deterministic spectral scale for that route:
\[
 e_{N,s}^{\mathrm{basic}}=\{\sqrt N(\lambda_s-\lambda_{s+1})\}^{-1},
 \qquad
 e_{N,s}^{\mathrm{ref}}=\left\{\frac{s^{2-q}\log(es)\log N}{N}\right\}^{1/2}.
\]
Then
\[
 \left\|\sup_t|(\widehat S-I)d_{t,s}|\right\|_{2,X\mid\mathscr X_{N,L}}
 =O_p(G_{r,s}e_{N,s}).
\]
This bound accounts for fitting the data-dependent representation
residual on the same pooled covariates used to learn the eigenspace.
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
Work on the event $\mathcal E_s$, whose probability tends to one, and
write $T=\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}$, $P=\Pi_{\le s}$, and
$Q=\widehat\Pi_{\le s}$. Set
\[
 A_{r,s}:=\sum_{j\le s}\lambda_j^{(r-1)/2}(e_j\otimes e_j),\qquad
 w_t:=\sum_{j\le s}a_j(t)e_j,\qquad
 V_{s,N}(x):=A_{r,s}(I-Q)K_x^0.
\]
Here $e_j=\sqrt{\lambda_j}\phi_j$, $g_{t,s}=A_{r,s}w_t$, and
$\sup_t\|w_t\|\le B_r$. Self-adjointness gives
\[
 d_{t,s}(x)=\langle w_t,V_{s,N}(x)\rangle,\qquad
 \sup_t|(\widehat S-I)d_{t,s}(x)|
 \le B_r\|(\widehat S-I)V_{s,N}(x)\|.
\]
Since $A_{r,s}P=A_{r,s}$ and
$\|A_{r,s}\|_\mathrm{op}=\lambda_s^{(r-1)/2}$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:source-representation-population}
 \|V_{s,N}\|_{L^2(\mathbb P_X;\mathcal H_{\kappa_0})}^2
 =\|A_{r,s}(P-Q)T^{1/2}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
 \le\lambda_s^{r-1}E_{N,s}^2.
\end{equation}
We next control its \emph{pooled empirical} norm; an iid expectation
identity cannot be applied to the data-dependent $V_{s,N}$.

Let $\widetilde T_N=P_N(K^0\otimes K^0)$, with
$P_Nf=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^Nf(X_i)$. Under the basic route,
$\|\widetilde T_N-T\|_\mathrm{op}=O_p(N^{-1/2})=o_p(\lambda_s)$ by
\hyperref[par:K3]{\textnormal{(K3)}}. Under the refined route,
Lemma~\ref{lem:derived-covariance-stability}, with
$\mu=\lambda_{s+1}\le\lambda_s$, gives
\[
 \widetilde T_N\preceq(1+\chi_{s,N})T+\chi_{s,N}\mu I,
 \qquad\chi_{s,N}=o_p(1).
\]
Thus in either case the event
$\widetilde T_N\preceq2(T+\lambda_s I)$ has probability tending to one.
On that event,
\[
 P_N\|V_{s,N}\|^2
 \le2\|A_{r,s}(I-Q)T^{1/2}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
       +2\lambda_s\|A_{r,s}(I-Q)\|_\mathrm{HS}^2.
\]
For the second term, the spectral decomposition of $T$ gives
\[
 \lambda_s\|P(I-Q)\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
 =\lambda_s\sum_{j\le s}\|(I-Q)e_j\|^2
 \le\sum_{j\le s}\lambda_j\|(I-Q)e_j\|^2
 \le E_{N,s}^2.
\]
Combining this with \eqref{eq:source-representation-population} yields
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:source-representation-pooled}
 P_N\|V_{s,N}\|^2\le4\lambda_s^{r-1}E_{N,s}^2
\end{equation}
on an event of probability tending to one.

Conditional on all pooled covariates, $V_{s,N}$ is fixed and the labeled
set is a uniform subset. Therefore the exact identity
\[
 \mathbb E_L[P_L\|V_{s,N}\|^2\mid X_1,\ldots,X_N]
       =P_N\|V_{s,N}\|^2
\]
holds, also when $N=n$. Since $E_{N,s}=O_p(e_{N,s})$, conditional
Markov on the good events in \eqref{eq:source-representation-pooled}
gives $P_L\|V_{s,N}\|^2=O_p(\lambda_s^{r-1}e_{N,s}^2)$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:hilbert-smoother-stability} now controls
$\|\widehat S V_{s,N}\|_{L^2}$ by the square root of this empirical
quantity, while \eqref{eq:source-representation-population} controls
the unfitted query residual. Multiplying their sum by $B_r$ proves the
claim. This argument never treats the learned projector or the labeled
inverse as independent of the data that define them.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{lem:sharp-fixed-design}
Under either spectral route and \hyperref[par:R1]{\textnormal{(R1)}},
\[
 \left\|\sup_t\left|\xi\mathbf q_L(\cdot)^\top
       \mathbf A_s\mathbf c_{t,s}\right|\right\|_{2,X\mid\mathscr X_{N,L}}
 =O_p\left(G_{r,s}\frac{\xi}{\lambda_s+\xi}\right).
\]
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
The common RKHS synthesis is an isometry on $\mathcal E_s$, so
$\sup_t\|\mathbf c_{t,s}\|\le G_{r,s}$.
The centered feature bound gives $\sup_x\|\mathbf q_L(x)\|\le4\sqrt{B_\kappa}$.
The labeled covariance sandwich and empirical eigenvalue comparability
imply $\|\mathbf A_s\|_\mathrm{op}=O_p((\lambda_s+\xi)^{-1})$.
Cauchy--Schwarz therefore proves the asserted bound even uniformly in
$x$. The exceptional event has vanishing probability. The rank-$s$
norm bound appears only in this ridge term and in the representation
term, not as a multiplier of the response-noise bound.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:contrast-error}
Under Conditions~\hyperref[par:M1]{\textnormal{(M1)}}, \hyperref[par:M2]{\textnormal{(M2)}}, and \hyperref[par:R1]{\textnormal{(R1)}}--\hyperref[par:R2]{\textnormal{(R2)}}, and
under either spectral route,
\[
 \mathbb E_{X^\circ}\left[R_{s,\xi}^{\oplus}(X^\circ)^2
          \mid\mathscr D_{n,N}\right]^{1/2}
 =O_p(\widetilde\rho_{r,s,n,N,\xi}).
\]
Under the source assumptions of
Proposition~\ref{prop:hilbert-automatic-complexity}, replace $b_{r,s}$
by $b^{\mathrm H}_{r,s}$ and $G_{r,s}$ by $G^{\mathrm H}_{r,s}$.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
On $\mathcal E_s$, Lemma~\ref{lem:fixed-design-decomposition} gives
four terms. Their indexed query norms are, respectively,
\[
 O_p\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\right),\qquad
 O_p(b_{r,s}),\qquad
 O_p(G_{r,s}e_{N,s}),\qquad
 O_p\!\left(G_{r,s}\frac{\xi}{\lambda_s+\xi}\right),
\]
by Lemmas~\ref{lem:sharp-response-noise},
\ref{lem:approximation-residual-bound},
\ref{lem:sharp-subspace-perturbation}, and \ref{lem:sharp-fixed-design}.
The last three terms depend only on the covariates and $L$, so their
query norms do not change when conditioning on $\mathscr D_{n,N}$
instead of $\mathscr X_{N,L}$. The supremum in $R_{s,\xi}^{\oplus}(x)$
is over a subset of $\mathfrak T$. Taking the supremum before applying
the $L^2$ triangle inequality therefore yields the sum of the four
bounds. Proposition~\ref{prop:primitive-spectral-verification} or
\ref{prop:sharp-spectral-verification} supplies the appropriate
$e_{N,s}$, producing the stated scale. Since $\mathbb P(\mathcal E_s^c)\to0$,
this is also the unconditional probability-order conclusion under the
specified fallback convention. The vector-valued source uses the
sharper residual calculation in
Proposition~\ref{prop:hilbert-automatic-complexity}; all other steps
are unchanged.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{rmk:approximation-tradeoff}
The model uses a single fixed kernel, not a sequence of learned RKHSs.
A target in $\overline{\mathcal H_{\kappa_0}}^{\,L^2(\mathbb P_X)}\setminus\mathcal H_{\kappa_0}$ is approximated
by $g_{t,s}=\Pi_{\le s}\dot h_t$; its RKHS norm may diverge as $s\to\infty$.
The source condition quantifies this through $G_{r,s}$ and the common
Hilbert-valued residual through $b_{r,s}$. It is stronger than pointwise
closure membership: the latter alone supplies neither uniform
approximation of the contrast family nor a convergence rate.

The use of $\ell^2$ in the residual proof does not impose a Hilbert
response space. It encodes the coefficients of scalar contrast
regressions. Lemma~\ref{lem:hilbert-smoother-stability} controls this
entire residual class and Lemma~\ref{lem:sharp-subspace-perturbation}
handles data reuse, rather than bounding an arbitrary residual family
by a $\sqrt{s}$-inflated envelope. The target remains the
true $m_\oplus$; no minimizer of an RKHS-projected criterion is substituted.
The theorems do not cover targets at positive distance from the closure,
and are not simultaneous guarantees for data-selected kernels, source
classes, or tuning sequences. Example~\ref{ex:bhv-closure-source}
verifies a non-RKHS BHV model, not every experimental generating law.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
\label{rmk:spectral-decay-regimes}
Let $\lambda_j\asymp j^{-q}$, $qr>1$, and use the refined route. The
squared source, variance, and representation terms are
\[
 s^{-(qr-1)},\qquad s/n,\qquad
 s^{2-qr}\log(es)\log N/N.
\]
Balancing the first two gives $s\asymp n^{1/(qr)}$; the sufficient
labeled condition $s^2/n\to0$ then requires $qr>2$. This is a limitation
of the present covariance-stability argument, not a necessity or a
minimax transition. No amount of unlabeled data removes that labeled
condition.

For $1<qr\le2$ and $N\ge n$, take an admissible complete-block cutoff
$s\asymp\sqrt n/(\log n)^2$ and the ridge choice in
Remark~\ref{rmk:ridge-sequence}. Then
$s\log(es)\sqrt{\log N/N}=O((\log n)^{-1/2})\to0$,
$s^2/n\to0$, and the bias dominates the other squared terms. Under
quadratic curvature this gives the valid, not claimed optimal, rate
\[
 \mathfrak R_{s,\xi,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}}
 =O_p\big(n^{-(qr-1)/2}(\log n)^{2(qr-1)}
          +\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}\big).
\]
Indeed the representation-to-bias ratio is bounded by
$s\log(es)\log N/N=o(1)$, and $s/n$ is no larger than the displayed
bias order. For other curvature exponents, substitute the corresponding
contrast scale into Theorem~\ref{thm:frechet-error}.

For the basic route with $N>n$, the automatic balanced cutoff instead
fails $\log(2s)/(n\lambda_s)\to0$ when $r\le1$. The basic theorem is
still useful at smaller ranks. For example, with a gap lower bound
$\lambda_s-\lambda_{s+1}\gtrsim s^{-q-1}$, choose $s\asymp n^c$,
$0<c<1/q$, and $N/s^{3q-qr+2}\to\infty$. Together with the same ridge
choice these conditions make all four basic error terms vanish. A
balanced-rate claim for that route would require an additional,
stronger approximation or design-stability argument.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
Let $p_M=2/(\beta_M-1)$. For a vanishing contrast scale
$\widetilde\rho_{r,s,n,N,\xi}$, the effective power in the prediction
bound is $p_M\wedge2$; quadratic curvature gives its square. Only an
$L^2$ query bound is used, including when $p_M>2$, because the inversion
inequality truncates that higher power on its small-error event.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
\label{rmk:ridge-sequence}
Under polynomial decay,
$G_{r,s}^2\lesssim s^{q(1-r)}$. For the general metric source, the
choice
\[
 0<\xi=O\big(s^{-(3q-1)/2}\big)
\]
ensures $\xi/\lambda_s\to0$ and
$\{G_{r,s}\xi/(\lambda_s+\xi)\}^2\lesssim s^{-(qr-1)}$.
For the vector-valued Hilbert source, use
$0<\xi=O(s^{-3q/2})$ to make the squared ridge term no larger than
$s^{-qr}$. These are sufficient deterministic choices, not statements
about validation-selected regularization.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
\label{cor:hilbert-fractional-rate}
Assume \hyperref[par:M1]{\textnormal{(M1)}}, \hyperref[par:M2]{\textnormal{(M2)}}--\hyperref[par:M3]{\textnormal{(M3)}}, \hyperref[par:K1]{\textnormal{(K1)}},
\hyperref[par:K4]{\textnormal{(K4)}}--\hyperref[par:K5]{\textnormal{(K5)}}, and the source conditions of
Proposition~\ref{prop:hilbert-automatic-complexity}, with $qr>1$.
Use the minimizer convention of Theorem~\ref{thm:frechet-error} and take
\[
 N\ge n,\qquad s\asymp n^{1/(qr+1)},\qquad
 0<\xi=O(s^{-3q/2}).
\]
Then the refined tuning conditions hold, \hyperref[par:R2]{\textnormal{(R2)}} is automatic, and
under the automatic quadratic curvature,
\[
 \mathfrak R_{s,\xi,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}}
 =O_p\big(n^{-qr/(qr+1)}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}\big).
\]
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
Proposition~\ref{prop:hilbert-automatic-complexity} and
Theorem~\ref{thm:contrast-error} give the squared scale
\[
 s^{-qr}+s/n+
 s^{q(1-r)}\{\xi/(\lambda_s+\xi)\}^2+
 s^{2-qr}\log(es)\log N/N.
\]
The first two terms balance at $n^{-qr/(qr+1)}$, and the chosen ridge
has the same or smaller order. Since $qr>1$, both
$s^2/n$ and $s\log(es)\sqrt{\log N/N}$ tend to zero. For large $n$,
$\log N/N\le\log n/n$ when $N\ge n$, and the representation-to-bias
ratio is at most $Cs^2(\log n)^2/n=o(1)$. The source supplies
\hyperref[par:R1]{\textnormal{(R1)}}, the proposition supplies \hyperref[par:R2]{\textnormal{(R2)}}, and the quadratic
argmin bound in Theorem~\ref{thm:frechet-error}, using the refined scale,
proves the result.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- lemma-23: body_md -->
\label{lem:uniform-linear-escape}
Under Conditions~\nameref{par:M1} and \nameref{par:M2}--\nameref{par:M3}, there exist
constants $\eta_0\in(0,\eta_M)$ and
$c_{\mathrm{esc}},R_0>0$ such that, uniformly in $x\in\mathcal X_0$,
\[
    \Delta_\oplus(y,x)
    \ge
    c_{\mathrm{esc}}d_{\mathcal Y}(y,m_\oplus(x))
\]
whenever $d_{\mathcal Y}(y,m_\oplus(x))\ge\eta_0$.
Moreover,
\[
 \Delta_\oplus(y,x)
 \ge\tfrac12d_{\mathcal Y}(y,m_\oplus(x))^2
\]
whenever \(d_{\mathcal Y}(y,m_\oplus(x))\ge R_0\).
<!-- end lemma-23 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
Fix $x\in\mathcal X_0$. Let \(y_*\) be the reference point in Condition~\nameref{par:M1}, and set
\[
    \sigma_*:=
    \sup_{x\in\mathcal X_0}M_\oplus(y_*,x)^{1/2}<\infty.
\]
Since \(m_\oplus(x)\) minimizes \(M_\oplus(\cdot,x)\),
\(M_\oplus(m_\oplus(x),x)\le M_\oplus(y_*,x)\). Minkowski's inequality also
gives
\[
    d_{\mathcal Y}(m_\oplus(x),y_*)
    \le M_\oplus(m_\oplus(x),x)^{1/2}
       +M_\oplus(y_*,x)^{1/2}
    \le2\sigma_*.
\]
Thus both the target and its conditional root-mean-square radius are uniformly
bounded by Condition~\nameref{par:M1}, which is the bound needed for the
escape argument.
Let $m_x=m_\oplus(x)$ and $r=d_{\mathcal Y}(y,m_x)$. Minkowski's inequality gives
\[
    M_\oplus(y,x)^{1/2}
    \ge r-M_\oplus(m_x,x)^{1/2}.
\]
Thus, whenever $r\ge4\sigma_*$,
\[
    \Delta_\oplus(y,x)
    \ge r^2-2r\sigma_*\ge\frac12r^2.
\]
Choose $\eta_0\in(0,\eta_M)$. Global separation gives
\[
    a_0
    :=
    \inf_{x\in\mathcal X_0}\inf_{d_{\mathcal Y}(y,m_x)\ge\eta_0}
    \Delta_\oplus(y,x)>0.
\]
Let $R_0:=4\sigma_*\lor\eta_0$. On
$\eta_0\le r\le R_0$, the excess is at least
$(a_0/R_0)r$. On $r\ge R_0$, it is at least
$(\eta_0/2)r$. Taking
$c_{\mathrm{esc}}=(a_0/R_0)\land(\eta_0/2)$ proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- lemma-24: body_md -->
\label{lem:normalized-argmin-inversion}
Assume Condition~\nameref{par:M3} and the conclusion of
Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform-linear-escape}. There exist constants
$C<\infty$ and $\eta_1\in(0,1]$, independent of $x$, the data, and the sample
sizes, with the following property. For $x\in\mathcal X_0$ and $0\le\eta\le\eta_1$,
suppose $\widehat m_x$ satisfies
\[
    \widehat M_{s,\xi}(\widehat m_x,x)
    \le
    \inf_{y\in\mathcal Y}\widehat M_{s,\xi}(y,x)+\eta.
\]
Put
\[
    m_x=m_\oplus(x),
    \qquad
    d_x=d_{\mathcal Y}(\widehat m_x,m_x),
    \qquad
    R_x=R_{s,\xi}^{\oplus}(x).
\]
Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:deterministic-inversion}
    d_x^2
    \le
    C\left[
        R_x^{2/(\beta_M-1)}
        \mathds{1}\{R_x<c_{\mathrm{esc}}/2\}
        +R_x^2\mathds{1}\{R_x\ge c_{\mathrm{esc}}/2\}
        +\eta^{2/\beta_M}
    \right].
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-24 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
Set $\eta_1:=1\land(c_{\mathrm{esc}}\eta_0/4)$ and take $0\le\eta\le\eta_1$.
If \(d_x=0\), then \eqref{eq:deterministic-inversion} is immediate. Assume
\(d_x>0\). Then \(t_x=(m_x,\widehat m_x)\in\mathfrak T\), and approximate
optimality gives
\[
 \widehat M_{s,\xi}(\widehat m_x,x)
 -\widehat M_{s,\xi}(m_x,x)\le\eta.
\]
By the definitions of \(R_x\), \(\widehat C_{s,\xi}\), and
\(\dot C_\oplus\),
\[
 \left|\{\widehat M_{s,\xi}(\widehat m_x,x)
 -\widehat M_{s,\xi}(m_x,x)\}
 -\Delta_\oplus(\widehat m_x,x)\right|
 \le R_xd_x.
\]
Combining the last two displays yields
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:approximate-basic-inequality}
    \Delta_\oplus(\widehat m_x,x)
    \le R_xd_x+\eta.
\end{equation}
If $R_x<c_{\mathrm{esc}}/2$ and $d_x\ge\eta_0$, the uniform escape bound
gives
\[
    c_{\mathrm{esc}}d_x
    \le R_xd_x+\eta
    <\frac{c_{\mathrm{esc}}}{2}d_x+\eta.
\]
Thus $d_x<2\eta/c_{\mathrm{esc}}\le\eta_0/2$, contradicting
$d_x\ge\eta_0$. Hence
$d_x<\eta_0$, and local curvature together with
\eqref{eq:approximate-basic-inequality} gives
\[
    c_Md_x^{\beta_M}\le R_xd_x+\eta.
\]
Young's inequality yields
\[
    R_xd_x
    \le
    \frac{c_M}{2}d_x^{\beta_M}
    +C R_x^{\beta_M/(\beta_M-1)}.
\]
Consequently,
\[
    d_x^{\beta_M}
    \le C\{R_x^{\beta_M/(\beta_M-1)}+\eta\}.
\]
Using $(u+v)^{2/\beta_M}\le
C_{\beta_M}(u^{2/\beta_M}+v^{2/\beta_M})$ for $u,v\ge0$ gives
\[
    d_x^2
    \le
    C\left\{R_x^{2/(\beta_M-1)}+\eta^{2/\beta_M}\right\},
\]
which proves the first part of \eqref{eq:deterministic-inversion}.

Now suppose $R_x\ge c_{\mathrm{esc}}/2$. If
$d_x<R_0$, then $d_x^2\le
4R_0^2c_{\mathrm{esc}}^{-2}R_x^2$. If
$d_x\ge R_0$, the quadratic lower bound in
Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform-linear-escape} and
\eqref{eq:approximate-basic-inequality} give
\[
    \frac12d_x^2\le R_xd_x+\eta
    \le\frac14d_x^2+R_x^2+\eta.
\]
Thus $d_x^2\le4R_x^2+4\eta$. Since
$R_x\ge c_{\mathrm{esc}}/2$ and $\eta\le1$,
\[
    4\eta
    \le \frac{16}{c_{\mathrm{esc}}^2}R_x^2.
\]
This proves the large-$R_x$ part with an explicit constant and
completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
Put
\[
    p_M:=\frac{2}{\beta_M-1},
    \qquad
    \rho:=\rho_{r,s,n,N,\xi},
    \qquad
    R_X:=R_{s,\xi}^{\oplus}(X^\circ).
\]
Since $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}\to0$, it satisfies
$0\le\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}\le\eta_1$ for all sufficiently large sample
sizes, where $\eta_1$ is from
Lemma~\ref{lem:normalized-argmin-inversion}. Apply that lemma for every
$x\in\mathcal X_0$ with $\eta=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}$. Since
$\mathbb P_X(\mathcal X_0)=1$ and $X^\circ$ is independent of the data, integrating over
$X^\circ$ gives the same prediction-risk inequality; no condition on the
conditional law outside $\mathcal X_0$ is used. Thus
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:frechet-risk-proof-integrated-inversion}
 \mathfrak R_{s,\xi,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}}
 \le C\bigg[
 \mathbb E_{X^\circ}[R_X^{p_M}
       \mathds{1}(R_X<c_{\mathrm{esc}}/2)\mid\mathscr D_{n,N}]
 +\mathbb E_{X^\circ}[R_X^2
       \mathds{1}(R_X\ge c_{\mathrm{esc}}/2)\mid\mathscr D_{n,N}]
 +\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}^{2/\beta_M}
 \bigg].
\end{equation}
Theorem~\ref{thm:contrast-error} states precisely that
\[
    \mathbb E_{X^\circ}[R_X^2\mid\mathscr D_{n,N}]^{1/2}
    =O_p(\rho).
\]

Suppose first that $p_M\le2$. Conditional Lyapunov inequality yields
\[
 \mathbb E_{X^\circ}[R_X^{p_M}\mid\mathscr D_{n,N}]
 \le
 \left\{\mathbb E_{X^\circ}[R_X^2\mid\mathscr D_{n,N}]\right\}^{p_M/2}
 =O_p(\rho^{p_M}),
\]
while the second expectation in
\eqref{eq:frechet-risk-proof-integrated-inversion} is bounded by
$O_p(\rho^2)$ using the preceding second-moment bound.

If $p_M>2$, then on $\{R_X<c_{\mathrm{esc}}/2\}$,
\[
    R_X^{p_M}
    \le(c_{\mathrm{esc}}/2)^{p_M-2}R_X^2.
\]
Hence both expectations in
\eqref{eq:frechet-risk-proof-integrated-inversion} are $O_p(\rho^2)$.
Combining the two cases, and retaining the harmless $\rho^{p_M}$ term in
both regimes, proves
\[
    \mathfrak R_{s,\xi,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}}
    =
    O_p\!\left(
      \rho^{2/(\beta_M-1)}+\rho^2
      +\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}^{2/\beta_M}
    \right).
\]
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- proof-38: body_md -->
Under the relative spectral route, Theorem~\ref{thm:contrast-error} gives
\[
    \mathbb E_{X^\circ}[R_{s,\xi}^{\oplus}(X^\circ)^2
      \mid\mathscr D_{n,N}]^{1/2}
    =O_p(\rho_{r,s,n,N,\xi}^{\star}).
\]
Repeating the pointwise inversion and conditional moment argument in the
proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:frechet-error}, with
$\rho_{r,s,n,N,\xi}^{\star}$ in place of
$\rho_{r,s,n,N,\xi}$, proves the stated rate.
<!-- end proof-38 -->
<!-- proof-39: body_md -->
Since $qr>2$, the chosen cutoff satisfies
\[
 \frac{s^2}{n}\asymp n^{2/(qr)-1}\to0,
 \qquad
 s\log(es)\sqrt{\frac{\log N}{N}}
 \lesssim n^{1/(qr)-1/2}(\log n)^{3/2}\to0
\]
for all $N\ge n$. The latter inequality uses that $u\mapsto\log u/u$
is decreasing for sufficiently large $u$. Also
\[
 \frac{\xi}{\lambda_s}=O(s^{-(q-1)/2})\to0.
\]
Thus all three refined tuning restrictions are verified, rather than
inferred from the desired error rate.

The source and labeled-noise terms satisfy
\[
 b_{r,s}^2+\frac{s}{n}
 \lesssim s^{-(qr-1)}+\frac{s}{n}
 \lesssim n^{-(qr-1)/(qr)}.
\]
The increasing source norm is retained explicitly in the ridge term:
\[
 \left(G_{r,s}\frac{\xi}{\lambda_s+\xi}\right)^2
 \lesssim s^{q(1-r)}s^{-(q-1)}
 =s^{-(qr-1)}.
\]
Similarly the squared representation term is bounded by
\[
 G_{r,s}^2\frac{s^{2-q}\log(es)\log N}{N}
 \lesssim \frac{s^{2-qr}\log(es)\log N}{N}.
\]
Its ratio to $s^{-(qr-1)}$ is at most
$Cs\log(es)\log N/N\lesssim n^{1/(qr)-1}(\log n)^2\to0$.
Using $(\sum_{j=1}^4u_j)^2\le4\sum_{j=1}^4u_j^2$ for nonnegative
$u_j$ now yields
\[
 (\rho_{r,s,n,N,\xi}^{\star})^2\lesssim n^{-(qr-1)/(qr)}.
\]
Theorem~\ref{thm:sharp-frechet-error} gives
\[
 \mathfrak R_{s,\xi,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}}
 =O_p\left(n^{-\frac{qr-1}{qr(\beta_M-1)}}
           +n^{-\frac{qr-1}{qr}}
           +\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}^{2/\beta_M}\right).
\]
When $\beta_M=2$ the first two terms have the same order, proving the
stated specialization.
<!-- end proof-39 -->
<!-- proof-40: body_md -->
Write $d=d_\mathcal Y$, $g_s=\lambda_s-\lambda_{s+1}$, and
$f_{n,N}=(N-n)/\{n(N-1)\}$. The source and kernel are fixed, and all
probability orders concern the joint law of the training data.

\@startsection{paragraph}{4}{\z@}{1.5ex plus
   0.5ex minus .2ex}{-1em}{\normalsize\bfseries}{1. Common-space representation of the fitted kernels.}
Put $Z_i=K_{X_i}^0$ and, for $B=L,[N]$, define
\[
 \overline Z_B=\frac1{|B|}\sum_{i\in B}Z_i,\qquad
 z_L(x)=K_x^0-\overline Z_L,\qquad
 \widehat T_B=\frac1{|B|}\sum_{i\in B}
           (Z_i-\overline Z_B)\otimes(Z_i-\overline Z_B).
\]
Let $\widehat\Pi_B$ denote the leading rank-$s$ projector of $\widehat T_B$ on
its empirical-gap event, with the zero fallback of
Appendix~\ref{app:empirical-spectral-construction} otherwise. Denote
its common-RKHS synthesis map by $\widehat E_s^B$; on the fallback
both this map and $\widehat\Pi_B$ are zero. Thus
$\widehat\Pi_B=\widehat E_s^B(\widehat E_s^B)^*$.
The empirical features have the representation
\[
 \widehat{\boldsymbol\Phi}_s^B(x)
   =(\widehat E_s^B)^*(K_x^0-\overline Z_B).
\]
Subtracting their labeled average cancels the representation-specific
centering exactly:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:th-proof-recentering}
 \mathbf q_L^B(x)=(\widehat E_s^B)^*z_L(x).
\end{equation}
This is the common-space identity already used in the original
spectral proof, applied separately to the two samples.
For $\alpha\in[0,1]$, set
\[
 W_\alpha(u,v)=\sqrt{1-\alpha}\,\widehat E_s^Lu
                    +\sqrt\alpha\,\widehat E_s^{[N]}v,
 \qquad Q_\alpha=W_\alpha W_\alpha^*
                    =(1-\alpha)\widehat\Pi_L+\alpha \widehat\Pi_{[N]}.
\]
The concatenated centered feature is $W_\alpha^*z_L(x)$, so its kernel
is $\langle z_L(x),Q_\alpha z_L(x')\rangle$. In particular,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:th-proof-kernel-difference}
 Q_\alpha-\widehat\Pi_{[N]}=(1-\alpha)(\widehat\Pi_L-\widehat\Pi_{[N]}),\qquad 0\preceq Q_\alpha\preceq\mathrm{Id}.
\end{equation}
This identity describes the sum kernel before the joint ridge refit;
it is not an average of two fitted coefficients. 
\@startsection{paragraph}{4}{\z@}{1.5ex plus
   0.5ex minus .2ex}{-1em}{\normalsize\bfseries}{2. Nested covariance and eigenspace comparison.}
For deterministic vectors $A_1,\ldots,A_N$ in any Hilbert space, let
$\overline A_B=|B|^{-1}\sum_{i\in B}A_i$. Sampling $L$ uniformly without
replacement gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:th-proof-finite-population}
 \mathbb E_L\!\left[\|\overline A_L-\overline A_{[N]}\|^2\right]
 =f_{n,N}\frac1N\sum_{i=1}^N\|A_i-\overline A_{[N]}\|^2.
\end{equation}
Indeed, for $a_i=A_i-\overline A_{[N]}$, one has $\sum_i a_i=0$,
$\mathbb P(i\in L)=n/N$, and
$\mathbb P(i,j\in L)=n(n-1)/\{N(N-1)\}$ for $i\ne j$. Expanding the
squared norm of $n^{-1}\sum_i\mathds{1}\{i\in L\}a_i$ and using
$\sum_{i\ne j}\langle a_i,a_j\rangle=-\sum_i\|a_i\|^2$ proves
\eqref{eq:th-proof-finite-population}.

Let $C_\kappa=\sup_{x\in\mathcal S}\|K_x^0\|^2\le4B_\kappa$ and
$\widetilde T_B=|B|^{-1}\sum_{i\in B}Z_i\otimes Z_i$.
Applying \eqref{eq:th-proof-finite-population}, conditional on all
pooled covariates, to $Z_i$ and then to the Hilbert--Schmidt operators
$Z_i\otimes Z_i$ yields
\[
 \mathbb E_L[\|\overline Z_L-\overline Z_{[N]}\|^2\mid X_1,\ldots,X_N]
 \le C_\kappa f_{n,N},\qquad
 \mathbb E_L[\|\widetilde T_L-\widetilde T_{[N]}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2\mid X_1,\ldots,X_N]
 \le C_\kappa^2 f_{n,N}.
\]
Since $\widehat T_B=\widetilde T_B-\overline Z_B\otimes\overline Z_B$,
$\|\overline Z_B\|\le\sqrt{C_\kappa}$, and
\[
 \|u\otimes u-v\otimes v\|_\mathrm{HS}
 \le(\|u\|+\|v\|)\|u-v\|,
\]
we obtain
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:th-proof-nested-covariance}
 \mathbb E_L[\|\widehat T_L-\widehat T_{[N]}\|_\mathrm{HS}^2\mid X_1,\ldots,X_N]
 \le10C_\kappa^2 f_{n,N}.
\end{equation}
Consequently, along sample sizes with $N>n$,
$\|\widehat T_L-\widehat T_{[N]}\|_\mathrm{op}=O_p(\sqrt{f_{n,N}})$.
This calculation does not treat the two covariance estimates as independent.

Put $A=\widehat T_{[N]}$, $B=\widehat T_L$,
$P=\widehat\Pi_{[N]}$, $P'=\widehat\Pi_L$, and
$\delta=\|A-B\|_\mathrm{op}$. Write $a_s,a_{s+1}$ for the pooled empirical
eigenvalues at the cutoff. The event
\[
 \mathcal G_{N,s}:=\{a_s-a_{s+1}\ge3g_s/4\}
\]
has probability tending to one under either spectral route. For the
basic route, Proposition~\ref{prop:primitive-spectral-verification}
and \hyperref[par:K3]{\textnormal{(K3)}} give
$\|A-\mathcal T_{\kappa_0}\|_\mathrm{op}=o_p(g_s)$, so Weyl's inequality
applies. For the refined route, use
Lemma~\ref{lem:empirical-cutoff-separation}. No corresponding
labeled-sample event is required.

We compare projectors on $\mathcal G_{N,s}$ in two cases, without a
dimension factor. If $\delta\le g_s/8$, Weyl's inequality gives a
labeled empirical gap of at least
$a_s-a_{s+1}-2\delta\ge g_s/2$. Both projectors therefore use their
leading spectral subspaces, not the zero fallback.
Let $A_\perp=A|_{\operatorname{ran}(\mathrm{Id}-P)}$ and
$B_+=B|_{\operatorname{ran}P'}$. Their spectra are separated by at
least $a_s-a_{s+1}-\delta\ge5g_s/8$. For
$X=(\mathrm{Id}-P)P'$ and $b=(a_s+a_{s+1})/2$, invariance gives
\[
 A_\perp X-XB_+=(\mathrm{Id}-P)(A-B)P'=:E,\qquad
 X=-\int_0^\infty e^{t(A_\perp-b\mathrm{Id})}
                  E e^{-t(B_+-b\mathrm{Id})}\,dt.
\]
The integral identity follows by differentiation; both exponential
factors decay. Hence
$\|(\mathrm{Id}-P)P'\|_\mathrm{op}\le8\delta/(5g_s)$.
Interchanging $A,B$ gives the same bound for
$(\mathrm{Id}-P')P$. Since
$P-P'=P(\mathrm{Id}-P')-(\mathrm{Id}-P)P'$, we obtain
$\|P-P'\|_\mathrm{op}\le16\delta/(5g_s)$.

If $\delta>g_s/8$, both $P,P'$ are orthogonal projections, including
the possible zero fallback for $P'$, so
$\|P-P'\|_\mathrm{op}\le1<8\delta/g_s$. Combining the two cases gives
\[
 \|\widehat\Pi_L-\widehat\Pi_{[N]}\|_\mathrm{op}
 \le\frac{8}{g_s}\|\widehat T_L-\widehat T_{[N]}\|_\mathrm{op}
 \quad\text{on }\mathcal G_{N,s}.
\]
Together with \eqref{eq:th-proof-nested-covariance} and
$\mathbb P(\mathcal G_{N,s}^c)\to0$, this proves
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:th-proof-projector-rate}
 \delta_\Pi:=\|\widehat\Pi_L-\widehat\Pi_{[N]}\|_\mathrm{op}
 =O_p\!\left(\frac{\sqrt{f_{n,N}}}{g_s}\right),\qquad N>n.
\end{equation}
If $N=n$, the two empirical covariances and their common-convention
projectors are identical, so $\delta_\Pi=0$ exactly. This handles
sequences that also contain sample sizes with $N=n$ without assigning
an ambiguous probability order to a zero normalizing sequence.

\@startsection{paragraph}{4}{\z@}{1.5ex plus
   0.5ex minus .2ex}{-1em}{\normalsize\bfseries}{3. Sensitivity of the actual ridge refit.}
Define $S:\mathcal H_{\kappa_0}\to\mathbb R^n$ by
$(Sh)_i=n^{-1/2}\langle z_L(X_i),h\rangle$, $i\in L$.
For a positive contraction $Q$, set
\[
 A_Q=SQS^*+\xi I_n,\qquad v_Q(x)=A_Q^{-1}SQz_L(x),\qquad
 \Gamma_\xi=\frac{2C_\kappa}{\xi}
                      \left(1+\frac{C_\kappa}{\xi}\right).
\]
For scalar observations $b_i$, put
$\overline b_L=n^{-1}\sum_{i\in L}b_i$ and
$b_c=n^{-1/2}(b_i-\overline b_L)_{i\in L}$.
If the centered features are $W^*z_L(x)$ and $WW^*=Q$, their centered
coefficient objective is
\[
 \min_\theta\ \frac12\|b_c-SW\theta\|_2^2+\frac\xi2\|\theta\|_2^2.
\]
The normal equations and the identity
$(F^*F+\xi I)^{-1}F^*=F^*(FF^*+\xi I)^{-1}$, with $F=SW$, give the
fitted scalar function
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:th-proof-scalar-fit}
 \widehat b_Q(x)=\overline b_L+\langle b_c,v_Q(x)\rangle_{\mathbb R^n}.
\end{equation}
This applies to $W=W_\alpha$ and to either endpoint synthesis map,
including the zero fallback, and keeps the cross-block regression
inverse in the concatenated fit.

The centered feature bounds give
\[
 \|S\|_\mathrm{op}^2\le\|S\|_\mathrm{HS}^2
 =n^{-1}\sum_{i\in L}\|Z_i\|^2-\|\overline Z_L\|^2\le C_\kappa,
 \qquad \sup_{x\in\mathcal S}\|z_L(x)\|\le2\sqrt{C_\kappa}.
\]
For $D=Q_1-Q_2$ and $R_j=(SQ_jS^*+\xi I_n)^{-1}$, positivity gives
$\|R_j\|_\mathrm{op}\le\xi^{-1}$ and the resolvent identity gives
$R_1-R_2=-R_1SDS^*R_2$. Consequently,
\[
 v_{Q_1}(x)-v_{Q_2}(x)
 =R_1SDz_L(x)-R_1SDS^*R_2SQ_2z_L(x),
\]
and, because $\|Q_2\|_\mathrm{op}\le1$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:th-proof-resolvent-bound}
 \sup_{x\in\mathcal S}\|v_{Q_1}(x)-v_{Q_2}(x)\|_2
 \le\left(\frac{2C_\kappa}{\xi}+\frac{2C_\kappa^2}{\xi^2}\right)
                          \|Q_1-Q_2\|_\mathrm{op}
 =\Gamma_\xi\|Q_1-Q_2\|_\mathrm{op}.
\end{equation}
These are deterministic identities and inequalities for the realized
data; they require no independence between the representations, the
fitting design, and the scalar observations.

\@startsection{paragraph}{4}{\z@}{1.5ex plus
   0.5ex minus .2ex}{-1em}{\normalsize\bfseries}{4. Uniform control of fitted normalized contrasts.}
For $t\in\mathfrak T$, write
\[
 \overline\psi_{t,L}=n^{-1}\sum_{i\in L}\psi_t(Y_i),\qquad
 \psi_{t,c}=n^{-1/2}(\psi_t(Y_i)-\overline\psi_{t,L})_{i\in L},\qquad
 U_L=\sup_{t\in\mathfrak T}\|\psi_{t,c}\|_2.
\]
Linearity of \eqref{eq:th-proof-scalar-fit} and constant reproduction
show that the normalized criterion fitted with $Q$ is
\[
 \widehat C_Q(t;x)
   =d_t+\overline\psi_{t,L}+\langle\psi_{t,c},v_Q(x)\rangle.
\]
Thus, by \eqref{eq:th-proof-kernel-difference} and
\eqref{eq:th-proof-resolvent-bound},
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:th-proof-contrast-discrepancy}
 \sup_{x\in\mathcal S}\sup_{t\in\mathfrak T}
 |\widehat C_{Q_\alpha}(t;x)-\widehat C_{\widehat\Pi_{[N]}}(t;x)|
 \le(1-\alpha)B_{n,N},\qquad B_{n,N}:=\Gamma_\xi U_L\delta_\Pi.
\end{equation}
All indexed suprema use the paper's measurable, separable versions.

To control $U_L$, put
$\sigma_*^2=\sup_{x\in\mathcal X_0}\mathbb E[d^2(Y,y_*)\mid X=x]$, which is finite
under \hyperref[par:M1]{\textnormal{(M1)}}. For $z=m_\oplus(x_z)$, minimization of the conditional
Fr\'echet function and conditional Minkowski give
$d(z,y_*)\le2\sigma_*$. Lemma~\ref{lem:directional-algebra} therefore
implies the common envelope
\[
 \sup_{t\in\mathfrak T}|\psi_t(v)|
 \le2d(v,y_*)+4\sigma_*.
\]
Empirical centering decreases each squared empirical norm, so
\[
 U_L^2\le n^{-1}\sum_{i\in L}\sup_{t\in\mathfrak T}|\psi_t(Y_i)|^2
 \le n^{-1}\sum_{i\in L}\{2d(Y_i,y_*)+4\sigma_*\}^2.
\]
The expectation of the last expression is at most $40\sigma_*^2$.
Markov's inequality gives $U_L=O_p(1)$. In particular, no interchange
of an indexed supremum and an expectation is being used. Since
$\Gamma_\xi\lesssim\xi^{-1}+\xi^{-2}$,
\eqref{eq:th-proof-projector-rate} yields
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:th-proof-B-rate}
 B_{n,N}=O_p(\chi_{s,n,N,\xi})\quad(N>n),
 \qquad B_{n,n}=0.
\end{equation}

\@startsection{paragraph}{4}{\z@}{1.5ex plus
   0.5ex minus .2ex}{-1em}{\normalsize\bfseries}{5. Population curvature, TH interpolation, and risk.}
Let $A_{\mathrm N}(x)=R_{s,\xi}^{\oplus}(x)$ be the pure pooled
normalized-contrast error from
Section~\ref{app:additional-rate-results}. For the RH fit define
\[
 A_\alpha(x)=\sup_{y\ne m_\oplus(x)}
 \left|\widehat C_{Q_\alpha}((m_\oplus(x),y);x)
             -\dot C_\oplus((m_\oplus(x),y);x)\right|.
\]
Then \eqref{eq:th-proof-contrast-discrepancy} gives
$A_\alpha(x)\le A_{\mathrm N}(x)+(1-\alpha)B_{n,N}$.
For any measurable $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}$-minimizer
$\widehat m^\alpha(x)$ of its own fitted criterion, let
$d_\alpha(x)=d(\widehat m^\alpha(x),m_\oplus(x))$.
Approximate optimality and the definition of the normalized error yield
\[
 \Delta_\oplus(\widehat m^\alpha(x),x)
 \le A_\alpha(x)d_\alpha(x)+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}.
\]
The population Hadamard variance inequality in
Proposition~\ref{prop:automatic-quadratic-curvature} bounds the left
side below by $d_\alpha(x)^2$. Solving this quadratic inequality gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:th-proof-endpoint-distance}
 d_\alpha(x)
 \le A_\alpha(x)+\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}}
 \le A_{\mathrm N}(x)+(1-\alpha)B_{n,N}
                            +\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}}.
\end{equation}
The same calculation applies at $\alpha=0,1$ to the labeled-only and
pooled endpoints, including separately selected approximate minimizers
with the common error bound. No convexity of these signed fitted
criteria has been assumed.

For two points $u,v$ in a Hadamard space, their constant-speed geodesic
$\gamma_{u,v}(\eta)$ minimizes
$F_\eta(y)=(1-\eta)d^2(y,u)+\eta d^2(y,v)$. Indeed the CAT(0)
inequality gives
\[
 F_\eta(y)\ge d^2(y,\gamma_{u,v}(\eta))
                  +\eta(1-\eta)d^2(u,v),\qquad
 F_\eta(\gamma_{u,v}(\eta))=\eta(1-\eta)d^2(u,v).
\]
It follows that every $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{hyb}}$-minimizer is within
$\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{hyb}}}$ of this geodesic point. Moreover,
convexity of distance along Hadamard geodesics gives
\[
 d(\gamma_{u,v}(\eta),m)
 \le(1-\eta)d(u,m)+\eta d(v,m).
\]
For completeness, the common-endpoint CAT(0) comparison bounds
$d(\gamma_{u,v}(\eta),\gamma_{u,m}(\eta))$ by $\eta d(v,m)$;
the triangle inequality and
$d(\gamma_{u,m}(\eta),m)=(1-\eta)d(u,m)$ give the displayed bound.

Apply these facts with $u=\widehat m^{\mathrm L}(x)$,
$v=\widehat m^{\alpha_{\mathrm H}}(x)$, and $m=m_\oplus(x)$.
Combining \eqref{eq:th-proof-endpoint-distance} at $\alpha=0$ and
$\alpha=\alpha_{\mathrm H}$ gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:th-proof-pointwise-bound}
 d(\widehat m^{\mathrm{TH}}_{\alpha_{\mathrm H},\eta}(x),m_\oplus(x))
 \le A_{\mathrm N}(x)+(1-\alpha_{\mathrm H}\eta)B_{n,N}
       +\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}}+\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{hyb}}},
\end{equation}
since $(1-\eta)+\eta(1-\alpha_{\mathrm H})=1-\alpha_{\mathrm H}\eta$.
Writing $a_{\mathrm N}=\|A_{\mathrm N}\|_{L^2(\mathbb P_X)}$, Minkowski's
inequality and the pooled endpoint bound imply
\begin{align}
 \sqrt{\mathfrak R(\widehat m^{\mathrm{TH}}_{\alpha_{\mathrm H},\eta})}
 &\le a_{\mathrm N}+(1-\alpha_{\mathrm H}\eta)B_{n,N}
       +\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}}+\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{hyb}}},
       \label{eq:th-proof-root-risk}\\
 \sqrt{\mathfrak D(\widehat m^{\mathrm{TH}}_{\alpha_{\mathrm H},\eta},\widehat m^{\mathrm N})}
 &\le 2a_{\mathrm N}+(1-\alpha_{\mathrm H}\eta)B_{n,N}
       +2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}}+\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{hyb}}}.
       \label{eq:th-proof-root-discrepancy}
\end{align}
The second inequality uses the triangle inequality through $m_\oplus$.
Theorem~\ref{thm:contrast-error}, under the hypotheses of the selected
basic or refined main theorem, gives $a_{\mathrm N}=O_p(\rho_{\mathrm N})$.
All preceding pathwise bounds hold simultaneously for every
$(\alpha_{\mathrm H},\eta)\in[0,1]^2$, using the joint selection and uniform
tolerances of Section~\ref{app:hybrid-extension-details}.
In particular, $a_{\mathrm N}$ and $B_{n,N}$ do not depend on the weights.
Squaring \eqref{eq:th-proof-root-risk}--\eqref{eq:th-proof-root-discrepancy}
gives, for every pair,
\[
 \mathfrak R(\widehat m^{\mathrm{TH}}_{\alpha_{\mathrm H},\eta})
 +\mathfrak D(\widehat m^{\mathrm{TH}}_{\alpha_{\mathrm H},\eta},\widehat m^{\mathrm N})
 \le20\{a_{\mathrm N}^2+(1-\alpha_{\mathrm H}\eta)^2B_{n,N}^2
                   +\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{hyb}}\}.
\]
Here $\rho_{\mathrm N}>0$ since it contains $\sqrt{s/n}$.
Put $V_{n,N}=B_{n,N}/\chi_{s,n,N,\xi}$ for $N>n$ and $V_{n,n}=0$;
the latter uses $B_{n,n}=0$. By \eqref{eq:th-proof-B-rate},
\[
 Z_{n,N}:=20\max\{1,(a_{\mathrm N}/\rho_{\mathrm N})^2,V_{n,N}^2\}
 =O_p(1).
\]
Under our measurable-version conventions, this is one measurable factor
bounding the ratio in \eqref{eq:th-stability-bound} for all weight pairs.
Thus no measurability of an uncountable supremum over selected fits is
needed. Substitution of any measurable, data-selected pair gives the
same bound under the joint law of training and validation data.
If $(1-\alpha_{\mathrm H}\eta)\chi_{s,n,N,\xi}=O_p(\rho_{\mathrm N})$, the stated
risk-order consequence follows.
<!-- end proof-40 -->
<!-- proposition-9: body_md -->
\label{prop:hybrid-geodesic-gain}
Let $\mathcal Y$ be Hadamard. Conditional on the data used to fit and select
measurable predictors $u,v$, let $(X^\circ,Y^\circ)\sim\mathbb P$ be an
independent test pair and define
\[
 \mathcal L(g):=\mathbb E[d_\mathcal Y^2(Y^\circ,g(X^\circ))\mid\text{fitted data}].
\]
Suppose $\mathcal L(u),\mathcal L(v)<\infty$. For $\eta\in[0,1]$, let
$g_\eta(x)$ be the exact geodesic point at fraction $\eta$ from $u(x)$
to $v(x)$. Then
\[
 \mathcal L(g_\eta)
 \le(1-\eta)\mathcal L(u)+\eta\mathcal L(v)
       -\eta(1-\eta)\mathfrak D(u,v).
\]
The same statement holds with $\mathfrak R$ in place of $\mathcal L$
when the endpoint target risks are finite. It applies to exact TH with
$(u,v)=(\widehat m^{\mathrm L},\widehat m^{\alpha_{\mathrm H}})$ and exact PH with
$(u,v)=(\widehat m^{\mathrm L},\widehat m^{\mathrm N})$, without
common-tuning or eigengap assumptions.
<!-- end proposition-9 -->
<!-- proof-41: body_md -->
Integrate the CAT(0) squared-distance inequality along the geodesic,
using $Y^\circ$ as the comparison point for $\mathcal L$, or
$m_\oplus(X^\circ)$ for $\mathfrak R$.
<!-- end proof-41 -->
<!-- proof-42: body_md -->
The labeled subset consists of $n$ iid labeled pairs under the sampling
scheme. Apply Corollary~\ref{cor:polynomial-rate} at the prescribed
common kernel, rank and ridge twice: with representation size $n$ for
$\widehat m^{\mathrm L}$ and size $N\ge n$ for $\widehat m^{\mathrm N}$.
Since $qr>2$, the refined tuning conditions hold for both sizes at
$s\asymp n^{1/(qr)}$. Hadamard curvature gives $\beta_M=2$, so both
endpoint risks are $O_p(a_n)$, where
$a_n=n^{-(qr-1)/(qr)}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}$.
Proposition~\ref{prop:hybrid-geodesic-gain} gives, simultaneously for
every $\eta\in[0,1]$,
\[
 \mathfrak R(\widehat m^{\mathrm{PH}}_\eta)
 \le(1-\eta)\mathfrak R(\widehat m^{\mathrm L})
      +\eta\mathfrak R(\widehat m^{\mathrm N})
 \le\max\{\mathfrak R(\widehat m^{\mathrm L}),
          \mathfrak R(\widehat m^{\mathrm N})\}=O_p(a_n).
\]
The maximum of these two risks is one measurable bound for every
mixing weight. Substituting a measurable validation-selected
$\widehat\eta$ therefore preserves the same order; no independence
between the endpoint fits or between a fit and $\widehat\eta$ is needed.
Prediction risk is evaluated at a fresh query independent of all
fitting and selection data. This proves the corollary without using
$\chi$ or a labeled eigengap condition.
<!-- end proof-42 -->
